(upper part of the heatmaps) or
\tilde{\eta}(0)
exceeds
2/\gamma
(right part of the heatmaps), and the lowest values of the risk are roughly parallel to the line
c\tilde{\eta}(0)=\ln(1/\gamma)
.
We remark that these scalings agree with the empirical practice in deep learning of using a sufficiently small clipping constant, with a learning rate renormalized by its value
(
De et al., 2022
;
McKenna et al., 2025
)
.

Figure 7:
Numerical simulations for
\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})
obtained via Algorithm
1
for
d=100
,
\zeta=0.3
,
(\theta^{*}_{i})^{2}=1/d
, and
\rho=0.1
, as a function of
n
. We fix
c=0.1
and consider the polynomial schedules in (
5.1
) for
\alpha\in\{0,0.5,1,2\}
(optimizing w.r.t.
\tilde{\eta}(0)
) and the harmonic schedule in (
5.18
) (optimizing w.r.t.
\beta
and
\tau
). We report the average over 10 independent trials, as well as the confidence interval corresponding to 1 standard deviation. We consider both isotropic data (
\Sigma=I
, first and third panel) and data with diagonal covariance whose eigenvalues are uniformly distributed in the interval
[0,2]
(second and fourth panel).

Effect of the learning rate schedule.

In Figures
7
and
8
, we compare different schedules for the learning rate
\tilde{\eta}(t)
, after setting
c=0.1
and optimizing
\tilde{\eta}(0)
numerically. We consider the polynomial schedules in (
5.1
) for
\alpha=\{0,0.5,1,2\}
and the harmonic schedule in (
5.18
).
The first and second panel in Figure
7
(which display the results for synthetic data) show that all schedules rapidly give better results as
n
increases, but larger values of
\alpha
are optimal only for large enough
n
. This effect is clearly shown in the third and fourth panel, which reports the same results normalized by the loss of output perturbation (
\alpha=0
).

Figure 8:
Numerical simulations for
\mathcal{P}(\theta^{p})
obtained via Algorithm
1
with
\rho=1
for the MNIST and the California housing dataset as a function of
n
. The method is in common with the one in Figure
7
.

Hence, if
n
is sufficiently small compared to
d
, output perturbation can outperform other schedules, in agreement with an observation made in
Dwork et al. (2025)
regarding the comparison between output and objective perturbation. Furthermore, the benefits of using larger values of
\alpha
for sufficiently large values of
n
seem more apparent in the case of non-isotropic covariance, displayed in the second and fourth panel.
The results for the harmonic schedule (reported in blue) are obtained after optimizing numerically
\beta
and
\tau
as in (
5.18
). The third panel of Figure
7
clearly displays the benefits of this choice for both small and large values of
n
. In the non-isotropic case (fourth panel), this schedule performs optimally for sufficiently small values of
n
, and it is on par with other polynomial schedules when
n=10^{4}d
(right part of the plot).
In Figure
8
, we run the same experiment for the MNIST and California housing datasets: we note that the harmonic schedule remains optimal, as suggested by our theory.

8
Discussion and future directions

We show optimal rates for DP linear regression in the proportional regime where the number of samples grows linearly with the input dimension.
To achieve this result, a crucial technical innovation is to establish a deterministic equivalent of the test risk of DP-GD via a family of deterministic ODEs, in the challenging setting where the clipping constant is of the order of the per-sample gradient. Analyzing the family of ODEs then gives bounds that are sharp enough to
(i)
demonstrate the benefit of small clipping constants, and to
(ii)
identify the optimal learning rate schedule.

The optimality of the rate
\gamma+\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}
holds for a well-conditioned covariance, as shown in the minimax lower bound of Theorem
3
. In the ill-conditioned setting, we consider a covariance spectrum distributed according to a power law (Assumption
3
), and we provide a tight analysis of the DP-GD risk for polynomially-decaying learning rate schedules. As in the well-conditioned case, our results highlight the benefits of small clipping constants and decaying schedules. However, in contrast to the well-conditioned case, establishing minimax rates remains an interesting direction for future work. We also note that the optimality of our rates holds when the variance of the label noise
\zeta^{2}
is a strictly positive constant independent of
\gamma
. We cautiously suspect that, for
\zeta=o_{\gamma}(1)
, the optimal rate is of order
\zeta^{2}(\gamma+\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2})
(see e.g. Theorem 4.1 in
Cai et al. (2021)
, where this dependence is tracked). In this regime, our bounds on
\mu_{c}(R)
and
\nu_{c}(R)
in Lemma
B.1
suggest that a sufficiently small value of the clipping constant is
c=O_{\gamma}(\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}})
rather than
c=O_{\gamma}(1)
. If
\zeta=o_{\gamma}(\gamma)
, setting a fixed
c=O_{\gamma}(\zeta)
might not allow to achieve the optimal rates (as in the right side of the panels in Figure
4
), suggesting the need for a time-dependent scheduling of
c
.
Interesting future directions also involve
(i)
proving that the optimal non-asymptotic rates read
d/n+d^{2}/(n\rho)^{2}
beyond the proportional regime, and
(ii)
better understanding the precise proportional asymptotics beyond the limit
\gamma\to 0
, for example showing the benefits of output perturbation w.r.t. schedules with decaying learning rate when
\gamma
is sufficiently large, as displayed in Figure
7
.

Finally, we remark that the technical approach of analyzing DP-GD through a deterministic equivalent can be used to study differentially private optimization beyond linear regression. A concrete setting for future work is provided e.g. by logistic regression, where the GD dynamics (in the absence of clipping and private noise) has been considered in
Collins-Woodfin and Seroussi (2025)
.

Appendix A
Proofs on differential privacy

The notion of zCDP can be converted to
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP, which in turn is defined below.

Definition A.1

(
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP
(
Dwork et al., 2006
)
)
.

A randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy if for any pair of adjacent datasets
D,D^{\prime}
, and for any subset of the parameters space
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}(D)\in S\right)\leq e^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}(D^{\prime})\in S\right)+\delta.

(A.1)

For completeness, we also provide the following definition.

Definition A.2

(Rényi DP
(
Mironov, 2017
)
)
.

Given
\alpha\in(1,+\infty)
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
, an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
Rényi DP if for any pair of adjacent datasets
D,D^{\prime}
we have
D_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}(D)\,\|\,\mathcal{A}(D^{\prime})\right)\leq\varepsilon
, where
D_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}(D)\,\|\,\mathcal{A}(D^{\prime})\right)
is the Rényi Divergence
(
Rényi, 1961
)
between the probability distributions induced by the randomness of
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,

D_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{A}(D)\,\|\,\mathcal{A}(D^{\prime})\right)=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\ln\int\left(\frac{p_{\mathcal{A}(D)}(\theta)}{p_{\mathcal{A}(D^{\prime})}(\theta)}\right)^{\alpha}p_{\mathcal{A}(D^{\prime})}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta.

(A.2)

Note that Definitions
3.1
and
A.2
imply that an algorithm is
\rho
-zCDP if, for any
\alpha>1
, it is also
(\alpha,\rho\alpha)
Rényi DP. The following proposition allows to translate Rényi DP and zCDP to
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.

Proposition A.3

(Proposition 1.3 in
Bun and Steinke (2016)
)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
satisfies
\rho^{2}/2
-zCDP, it also satisfies
\left(\rho^{2}/2+\rho\sqrt{2\ln(1/\delta)},\delta\right)
-DP, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Then, if we consider
\delta
such that
\rho\leq\sqrt{\ln(1/\delta)}
, we achieve
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP if we have

\rho^{2}/2+\rho\sqrt{2\ln(1/\delta)}\leq 2\rho\sqrt{\ln(1/\delta)}\leq\varepsilon,

(A.3)

which means that for algorithms respecting
\rho^{2}/2
-zCDP, we can replace
2\rho
by
\varepsilon/\sqrt{\ln(1/\delta)}
in the error bounds to evaluate the cost of privacy in terms of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.

A.1
Proof of Proposition
4.1

Let us define the family of functions
\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, for all
k\in[n]
, such that
\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(0)=0
, and

\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}^{\prime}(z)=z\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{C_{\textup{clip}}}{\left|z\right|{\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}}}\right).

(A.4)

In words,
\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(z)
is a quadratic function, but linearized for sufficiently large values of
|z|
, such that it is
C_{\textup{clip}}/\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}
-Lipschitz. Then, we have

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{k}

\displaystyle=g_{k}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{C_{\textup{clip}}}{\left\|g_{k}\right\|_{2}}\right)

(A.5)

\displaystyle=x_{k}\left(x_{k}^{\top}\theta_{k-1}-y_{k}\right)\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{C_{\textup{clip}}}{\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}\left|x_{k}^{\top}\theta_{k-1}-y_{k}\right|}\right)

\displaystyle=x_{k}\ell^{\prime}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}\left(x_{k}^{\top}\theta_{k-1}-y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}\left(x_{k}^{\top}\theta_{k-1}-y_{k}\right),

where the first step follows from the definition of
\bar{g}_{k}
in Algorithm
1
. Furthermore, for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\theta}\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta-y_{k})-\nabla_{\theta}\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta^{\prime}-y_{k})\right\|_{2}

(A.6)

\displaystyle=\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}\left|\ell^{\prime}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta-y_{k})-\ell^{\prime}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta^{\prime}-y_{k})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}\left|x_{k}^{\top}\left(\theta-\theta^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\right\|_{2},

where the second step follows from the fact that
\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}^{\prime}(z)
is a 1-Lipschitz function. Let us now define

\bar{\ell}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(z)=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{2}{\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}\eta_{k}}\right)\ell_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(z).

(A.7)

Then, we have that every iteration of Algorithm
1
takes the form

\theta_{k}=\theta_{k-1}-\eta_{k}\nabla_{\theta}\bar{\ell}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta_{k-1}-y_{k})+2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{k}b_{k},

(A.8)

where
\bar{\ell}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta-y_{k})
is
C_{\textup{clip}}
-Lipschitz with respect to
\theta
, and it is
2/\eta_{k}
-smooth, i.e.,

\left\|\nabla_{\theta}\bar{\ell}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta-y_{k})-\nabla_{\theta}\bar{\ell}_{k,C_{\textup{clip}}}(x_{k}^{\top}\theta^{\prime}-y_{k})\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{2}{\eta_{k}}\left\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\right\|_{2},

(A.9)

due to (
A.6
) and (
A.7
).
Thus, the desired result follows from Theorem 3.1 in
Feldman et al. (2020)
, after setting their batch sizes
\{B_{k}\}
identically equal to 1, and their projection set
\mathcal{K}
equal to all
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. ∎

Appendix B
Auxiliary lemmas on
\mu_{c}(\theta)
and
\nu_{c}(\theta)

Lemma B.1
.

Let Assumption
1
hold, and let
\mu_{c}(\theta)
and
\nu_{c}(\theta)
be defined according to (
4.6
). Then, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{c}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{c}{2\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\right),

(B.1)

\displaystyle\nu_{c}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}}{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\left(1-\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{c}{2\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\right)\right)+F\left(\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\right),

(B.2)

where

\mathrm{erf}(z)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{z}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t,\qquad F(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-z}^{z}t^{2}e^{-t^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}t=\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2}}\right)-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}ze^{-z^{2}/2}.

(B.3)

In particular,
\mu_{c}(\theta)
and
\nu_{c}(\theta)
depend only on
c
and the test risk
\mathcal{P}(\theta)
via the ratio
c/\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}
.

Proof.

Recall that

r(\theta,x,y)=x^{\top}\theta-y,\qquad r_{c}(\theta,x,y)=r(\theta,x,y)\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{c}{\left|r(\theta,x,y)\right|}\right),

(B.4)

\mu_{c}(\theta)=\frac{\left\|\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r_{c}(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]\right\|_{2}}{\left\|\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]\right\|_{2}},\qquad\nu_{c}(\theta)=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r_{c}(\theta,x,y)^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r(\theta,x,y)^{2}\right]}.

(B.5)

Until the end of the proof, we will use the notation
\textup{clip}_{c}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
to denote the function such that

\textup{clip}_{c}(a)=a\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{c}{\left|a\right|}\right).

(B.6)

In particular,
r_{c}(\theta,x,y)=\textup{clip}_{c}\left(r(\theta,x,y)\right)
.

Let us look at the first entry of the vector
\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r_{c}(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]
,

\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r_{c}(\theta,x,y)x^{\top}e_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\rho_{1})\rho_{2}\right],

(B.7)

where the second step introduced
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
, defined as two mean-0 Gaussian random variables, such that

\mathrm{Var}(\rho_{1})=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\theta-\theta^{*})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\zeta^{2},\qquad\mathrm{Var}(\rho_{2})=\Sigma_{11},\qquad\mathrm{Cov}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=e_{1}^{\top}\Sigma(\theta-\theta^{*}).

(B.8)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\rho_{1})\rho_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Cov}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}{\mathrm{Var}(\rho_{1})}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{1}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\rho_{1})\rho_{1}\right]

(B.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Cov}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\rho_{1})}}\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\rho_{1})}\hat{\rho})\hat{\rho}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{1}^{\top}\Sigma(\theta-\theta^{*})}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\hat{\rho})\hat{\rho}\right],

where we used
\mathcal{P}(\theta)=\mathcal{R}(\theta)+\zeta^{2}/2=\mathrm{Var}(\rho_{1})/2
and we introduced the standard Gaussian random variable
\hat{\rho}
. As this argument holds for any component of the vector
\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r_{c}(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]
, plugging the equation above in (
B.7
) gives

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r_{c}(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\|\Sigma(\theta-\theta^{*})\right\|_{2}}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\hat{\rho})\hat{\rho}\right]

(B.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\|\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]\right\|_{2}}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\hat{\rho})\hat{\rho}\right],

where in the second step we used that
\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\left[r(\theta,x,y)\,x\right]=\Sigma(\theta-\theta^{*})
. Then, we also have

\mu_{c}(\theta)=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\hat{\rho})\hat{\rho}\right]}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}.

(B.11)

Defining the shorthand
c^{\prime}(\theta)=c/\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}
, the numerator of the expression above yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\hat{\rho})\hat{\rho}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}{\hat{\rho}}^{2}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}+\frac{2c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{+\infty}\hat{\rho}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}

(B.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\left.-\hat{\rho}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\right|_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}+\int_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}\right)-\frac{2c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left.e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\right|_{c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{+\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(-2c^{\prime}(\theta)e^{-{c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{2}/2}+\int_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}\right)+\frac{2c}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-{c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{2}/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)/\sqrt{2}}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\,\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{c}{\sqrt{4\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\right),

which, plugged in (
B.11
), gives the first part of the thesis.

For the second part of the thesis, following the same argument we used to write (
B.11
), we have

\nu_{c}(\theta)=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\rho}}\left[\textup{clip}_{c}(\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\hat{\rho})^{2}\right]}{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)},

(B.13)

where, as before,
\hat{\rho}
denotes a standard Gaussian random variable. Then, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{c}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}{\hat{\rho}}^{2}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}+\frac{2c^{\prime}(\theta)^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{+\infty}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}

(B.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-c^{\prime}(\theta)}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}{\hat{\rho}}^{2}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}+c^{\prime}(\theta)^{2}\left(1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{c^{\prime}(\theta)}e^{-{\hat{\rho}}^{2}/2}\mathrm{d}\hat{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{c^{2}}{2\mathcal{P}(\theta)}\left(1-\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{c}{2\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(\theta)}}\right)\right)+F(c^{\prime}(\theta)),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let Assumption
1
hold, and let
\mu_{c}(R)
and
\nu_{c}(R)
be defined according to (
4.6
), where
R
denotes a generic value of the test risk, since
\mu_{c}(\theta)
and
\nu_{c}(\theta)
depend only on
c
and the test risk
\mathcal{P}(\theta)
due to Lemma
B.1
. Then, for any
c>0
, we have that

\underline{c}_{\mu}(c,\zeta)<\frac{\mu_{c}(R)\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}{c}<\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}},\qquad\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)<\frac{\nu_{c}(R)(2R+\zeta^{2})}{c^{2}}<1,

(B.15)

where
\underline{c}_{\mu}(c,\zeta)
and
\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)
denote two positive constants which depend on the values of
c
and
\zeta
and are monotonously decreasing in
c
.

We also have that, as
c/\zeta\to 0
,

\left|\frac{\mu_{c}(R)\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}{c}-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\right|=o_{c/\zeta}\left(1\right),\qquad\left|\frac{\nu_{c}(R)\left(2R+\zeta^{2}\right)}{c^{2}}-1\right|=o_{c/\zeta}\left(1\right).

(B.16)

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{c}(R)(2R+\zeta^{2})}{c^{2}}>\frac{1}{2},\qquad\text{if }\frac{c}{\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}\leq 1,

(B.17)

\displaystyle\nu_{c}(R)>\frac{1}{2},\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\,\,\text{if }\frac{c}{\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}>1.

Figure 9:
The functions
\mu_{c}(R)
,
\mu_{c}(R)/c^{\prime}
,
\nu_{c}(R)
,
\nu_{c}(R)/c^{\prime}
, plotted as a function of
c^{\prime}=c/\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}
.

Proof.

Note that, introducing the notation

c^{\prime}=\frac{c}{\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}\leq\frac{c}{\zeta},

(B.18)

we have that

\mu_{c}(R)=\mathrm{erf}\left(c^{\prime}/\sqrt{2}\right)<1,

(B.19)

and

\nu_{c}(R)=(c^{\prime})^{2}\left(1-\mathrm{erf}\left(c^{\prime}/\sqrt{2}\right)\right)+F(c^{\prime})<1,

(B.20)

where the last inequalities can be verified directly via the definitions in (
4.6
).
Furthermore, we have that both
\mu_{c}(R)
and
\nu_{c}(R)
are increasing functions of
c^{\prime}
, equal to
0
when
c^{\prime}=0
. This follows from the definition for
\mu_{c}(R)
, and can be promptly verified for
\nu_{c}(R)
via derivation.

We further have that, for
c^{\prime}>0
,

\frac{\mu_{c}(R)\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}{c}=\frac{1}{c^{\prime}}\mathrm{erf}\left(c^{\prime}/\sqrt{2}\right)<\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}},

(B.21)

and

\frac{\nu_{c}(R)\left(2R+\zeta^{2}\right)}{c^{2}}=\left(1-\mathrm{erf}\left(c^{\prime}/\sqrt{2}\right)\right)+\frac{F(c^{\prime})}{(c^{\prime})^{2}}<1,

(B.22)

where both the LHSs are decreasing functions of
c^{\prime}
, going to
0
for
c^{\prime}\to+\infty
(this can be seen via explicit derivation with respect to
c^{\prime}
, and via the identity
0\geq\int_{0}^{z}-2t^{2}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t=ze^{-z^{2}}-\int_{0}^{z}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t
), and where the last inequalities can be verified computing the limit for
c^{\prime}\to 0^{+}
via l’Hôpital rule, which gives

\lim_{c^{\prime}\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mu_{c}(R)\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}{c}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}},\qquad\lim_{c^{\prime}\to 0^{+}}\frac{\nu_{c}(R)\left(2R+\zeta^{2}\right)}{c^{2}}=1.

(B.23)

Note that, as
R\geq 0
, the above limit is achieved when
c/\zeta\to 0
. Then, due to the inequality in (
B.18
), the first and second part of the thesis follow.

Note that, for
c^{\prime}=1
, we have

\nu_{c}(R)=1-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi e}}\approx 0.516.

(B.24)

Thus, the third and fourth part of the thesis follow from the monotonicity of
\nu_{c}(R)/c^{\prime}
and
\nu_{c}(R)
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
4

We will use the notation
\|Z\|_{\psi_{p}}=\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{E}\exp(|Z|^{p}/t^{p})\leq 2\}
to denote the Orlicz norm of a random variable
Z
for any
p\geq 1
. We denote the inner product between two vectors
a
and
b
as
\langle{a,b}\rangle=a^{\top}b
(with notation applicable to matrices
\langle{aa^{\top},A}\rangle=a^{\top}Aa
). Given two real-valued quantities
a,b
, we denote by
a\wedge b=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(a,b)
. Given a quadratic functions
q:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
we define its
\|\cdot\|_{C^{2}}
norm such that
\|q\|_{C^{2}}=\|\nabla^{2}q\|_{\text{op}}+\|\nabla q(0)\|_{2}+|q(0)|
. Furthermore, we will denote with
\gamma_{n}
the ratio
d/n
for a fixed value of
n
.

We start by proving a general result about homogenized DP-GD, informally defined in Remark
4.3
and formally defined below.

Definition C.1

(Homogenized DP-GD)
.

For any
t\in[0,1)
, we define
homogenized DP-GD
(H-DP-GD) as the solution of the SDE

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Theta_{t}=-2\bar{\eta}(t){\mu_{c}(\Theta_{t})}\nabla\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\eta}(t)\sqrt{\frac{2\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\Sigma}{n}}\mathrm{d}B^{s}_{t}+2\frac{\sqrt{d}}{n}c\tilde{\sigma}(t)\mathrm{d}B^{p}_{t},

(C.1)

where
\Theta_{0}=\theta_{0}=0
,
B^{s}_{t}
and
B^{p}_{t}
are two independent standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\bar{\eta}(t)=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(\tilde{\eta}(t),2n/d)
, and
\tilde{\sigma}(t)
is such that
\rho^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)=-\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)/\mathrm{d}t
.

Theorem
5
below shows that DP-GD is close to H-DP-GD over the class of quadratic functions
Q
, given by

\displaystyle Q=\{q:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}\;\textup{ s.t .}\;q(v)=v^{\top}R(z;\Sigma)v/2,\;\textup{ with }\;z\in\Omega\},

(C.2)

where
R(z;\Sigma)=(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}
is the resolvent matrix of
\Sigma
and
\Omega:=\left\{w\in\mathbb{C}:|w|=2\|\Sigma\|_{\text{op}}\right\}
.

Theorem 5
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Let
\rho=\Theta(1)
,
n,d\to\infty
s.t.
d/n\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
.
Denote by
\Theta_{t}
and
\theta_{k}
independent realizations of H-DP-GD (as per Definition
C.1
) and Algorithm
1
.
Then, with overwhelming probability, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left|q(\Theta_{t}-\theta^{*})-q(\theta_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}-\theta^{*})\right|=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

(C.3)

uniformly for all functions
q\in Q
.

Proof.

Consider the notation
u_{k}=\theta_{k}-\theta^{*}
. We have the following update rule for
k\in[n]
:

\displaystyle u_{k}=u_{k-1}-\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{g}_{k}+2c\sqrt{d}\sigma_{k}b_{k},

(C.4)

where we recall that

\bar{\eta}_{k}=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(\eta_{k},\frac{2}{\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}}\right),\qquad\bar{g}_{k}=g_{k}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(1,\frac{c\sqrt{d}}{\|g_{k}\|_{2}}\right),\qquad g_{k}=\left(\langle{x_{k},u_{k}}\rangle-z_{k}\right)x_{k}.

(C.5)

Let
q:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a generic quadratic function. Then, using a Taylor expansion, the update rule for
q(u_{k})
reads

\displaystyle q(u_{k+1})

\displaystyle=q(u_{k})-{\bar{\eta}_{k}}\bar{g}_{k}^{\top}\nabla q(u_{k})+2c\sqrt{d}\sigma_{k}b_{k}^{\top}\nabla q(u_{k})

(C.6)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(({2c\sqrt{d}\sigma_{k}b_{k}}-\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{g}_{k})^{\otimes 2}\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})\right).

Defining the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{k}:=\sigma(\{u_{i}\}_{i=0}^{k})
generated by the iterates of DP-GD in (
C.6
), we write the Doob’s decomposition of the above process:

\displaystyle q(u_{k+1})-q(u_{k})

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\bar{\eta}(k/n)}}{n}\mu_{c}(u_{k})\langle{\Sigma u_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle

(C.7)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{{\bar{\eta}^{2}(k/n)}}{{2}n}\nu_{c}(u_{k})\mathcal{P}(u_{k})\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}))+\frac{1}{n}\frac{2d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(k/n)^{2}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}))

\displaystyle\qquad+\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Grad}}(q)+\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Hess}}(q)+\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Hess}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)+\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]+\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Step}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}],

where we recall the notation
\bar{\eta}(k/n)=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(\tilde{\eta}(k/n),2/\gamma_{n}\right)
,
\eta_{k}=\tilde{\eta}(k/n)/n
, and where we introduced the shorthands

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Grad}}(q)

\displaystyle:=-\bar{\eta}_{k}\langle{\bar{g}_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle+\bar{\eta}_{k}\mu_{c}(u_{k})\langle{\Sigma u_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle

(C.8)

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Hess}}(q)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\langle{(\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{g}_{k})^{\otimes 2},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbb{E}[(\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{g}_{k})^{\otimes 2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}],\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Hess}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbb{E}[(\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{g}_{k})^{\otimes 2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}],\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{n\bar{\eta}_{k}^{2}}{2}\nu_{c}(u_{k})\mathcal{P}(u_{k})\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}))

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)

\displaystyle:=2c\sqrt{{d}}{\sigma}_{k}\langle{b_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\langle{2c\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}{\sigma}_{k}b_{k}{n\bar{\eta}_{k}}\bar{g}_{k}^{\top},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{d}{2}\langle{(2c{\sigma}_{k}b_{k})^{\otimes 2},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle-2dc^{2}\langle{{\sigma}_{k}^{2}I_{d},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle:={2d}c^{2}\langle{\left(\sigma_{k}^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{3}}\tilde{\sigma}(k/n)^{2}\right)I_{d},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})}\rangle,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Step}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/d)}{n}-\bar{\eta}_{k}\right)\mu_{c}(u_{k})\langle{\Sigma u_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{\bar{\eta}_{k}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/n)^{2}}{2n^{2}}\right)\nu_{c}(u_{k})\mathcal{P}(u_{k})\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})).

We note that the first three terms are in common with the analysis in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, while the last three are the result of the private noise and the adaptive learning rate step in Algorithm
1
.

In a similar way, we introduce the shorthand
V_{t}=\Theta_{t}-\theta^{*}
such that
V_{0}=u_{0}
. Using Itô’s formula on (
4.14
), for any quadratic function
q
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}q(V_{t})=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(V_{t})\langle{\Sigma V_{t},\nabla q(V_{t})}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(V_{t})\mathcal{P}(V_{t})\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q(V_{t})\right)\mathrm{d}t

(C.9)

\displaystyle+2\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\nabla^{2}q(V_{t})\right)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}},

where we introduced the shorthand

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}}:=\langle{\nabla q(V_{t}),\sqrt{\frac{2\bar{\eta}(t)^{2}\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\Sigma}{n}+4\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(t)^{2}I_{d}}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}}\rangle,

(C.10)

with
B_{t}
being a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion.

Let
M
be a positive constant that will be fixed later. The dynamic is first controlled up to the stopping time

\tau:=\inf\{k:\|u_{k}\|_{2}\geq M\cup\lfloor tn\rfloor:\|V_{t}\|_{2}\geq M\}.

(C.11)

Then, we will denote the stopped processes
u_{k}^{\tau}=u_{k\wedge\tau}
and
V_{t}^{\tau}=V_{t\wedge(\tau/n)}
, which will be the objects we will compare in the following arguments. This stopping time is introduced for technical reasons, and we will later show that
\tau\geq n
.

Taking the difference between (
C.7
) and (
C.9
),
and following the same argument in Lemma 1 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, we get that
there exists an absolute constants
C_{1}=C_{1}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}},c,\bar{\eta})
, such that

\sup_{0\leq t<1}\left|q(u_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\tau})-q(V_{t}^{\tau})\right|\leq C_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\sup_{q\in Q}\left|q(u_{\lfloor sn\rfloor}^{\tau})-q(V_{s}^{\tau})\right|\mathrm{d}s\\
+\sup_{0\leq t<(1\wedge(\tau/n))}\left(|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Hess}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|+|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|+|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\text{Step}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|\right)\\
+\sup_{0\leq t<(1\wedge(\tau/n))}\left(|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\textup{Grad}}(q)|+|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\textup{Hess}}(q)|+|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)|+|\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\textup{H-DP-GD}}(q)|\right)+O(d^{-1}),

(C.12)

where we introduced the notation
\mathcal{M}_{k}(q)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Delta\mathcal{M}_{j}(q)
and
\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\textup{H-DP-GD}}(q)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{s}^{\textup{H-DP-GD}}(q)
. The last term follows from transitioning (
C.7
) to continuous times, which involves an additional discretization error of
O(d^{-1})
(see also Section A.3 in
Collins-Woodfin et al. (2024a)
for more details). Importantly, differently from Lemma 1 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, we miss a term proportional to
\left(\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{s})+\mathcal{R}(\theta_{\lfloor sn\rfloor})\right)^{-1/2}
in (
C.12
). This is possible since, as
\zeta>0
, both
\mu_{c}(\theta)
and
\nu_{c}(\theta)\mathcal{P}(\theta)
are Lipschitz with respect to
\mathcal{R}(\theta)
with constant-order Lipschitz constant due to Lemma
B.2
, strengthening the condition in their Assumption 5.

Denoting with
\mathcal{M}
the sum of the last two lines in (
C.12
), by Lemma 2 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
(for
|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\textup{Grad}}(q)|
,
|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\textup{Hess}}(q)|
and
|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Hess}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|
), Lemma
C.2
(for
|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)|
and
|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|
), Lemma
C.3
(for
|\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\textup{H-DP-GD}}(q)|
), and Lemma
C.5
(for
|\mathcal{M}_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\text{Step}}(q)|
), there are two constants
C_{2}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}},\bar{\eta},M,c,\gamma)
and
C_{3}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}},\bar{\eta},M,c)>0
such that

\mathcal{M}\leq C_{2}n^{-1/2}\left(\log^{2}n+C_{3}\right),

(C.13)

with probability at least
1-e^{-\log^{2}n}
.

Then, following Lemma 3 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, we can define a set
\bar{Q}\subseteq Q
with
|\bar{Q}|\leq C_{4}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}})d^{4}
, such that, for all
q\in Q
, there exists a
\bar{q}\in\bar{Q}
that satisfies
\|q-\bar{q}\|_{C^{2}}\leq d^{-2}
. Then, taking the union bound over this set yields

\sup_{q\in Q}\sup_{0\leq t<1}\left|q(u_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\tau})-q(V_{t}^{\tau})\right|\leq C_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\sup_{q\in Q}\left|q(u_{\lfloor sn\rfloor}^{\tau})-q(V_{s}^{\tau})\right|\mathrm{d}s+\mathcal{M}\,+\,C_{5}d^{-2},

(C.14)

with probability at least
1-C_{4}d^{4}e^{-\log^{2}n}
. Thus, with this same probability, the application of Gronwall’s inequality gives

\sup_{q\in Q}\sup_{0\leq t<1}\left|q(u_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}^{\tau})-q(V_{t}^{\tau})\right|\leq\mathcal{(}\mathcal{M}+C_{5}d^{-2})\exp\left(C_{1}\right)=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

(C.15)

where the last step hides a dependence on
M
(see (
C.11
)). Then, we are left to show that
\tau\geq n
for
M
chosen to be a constant independent of
d
and
n
. This follows the same approach as in Lemma 4 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, which is here formalized in Lemma
C.4
. In particular, in Lemma
C.4
, we show that there exists a constant
C_{6}(\|\Sigma\|_{\text{op}},c,\bar{\eta})>0
, such that for any
r\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-r^{2}/2}
, it holds that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|u_{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\|^{2}\leq\|u_{0}\|^{2}e^{C_{6}(1+d^{-1/2}r)}
and
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|V_{t}\|^{2}\leq\|V_{0}\|^{2}e^{C_{6}(1+d^{-1/2}r)}
. Thus,
M
can be chosen as a constant independent from
d
and
n
, such that
\tau>n
with overwhelming probability, and the thesis follows.
∎

Proof of Remark
4.3
.

The result follows the same proof of Theorem
5
, but considering
q(\theta-\theta^{*})=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\theta-\theta^{*})\right\|_{2}^{2}/2
. While this function is not included in
Q
, it is still such that
\left\|q\right\|_{C^{2}}=O(1)
, allowing the same argument to go through. Alternatively, it can be recovered by the Cauchy integral formula taking
\frac{i}{4\pi}\oint_{\Omega}zq(\cdot)\mathrm{d}z
(see, e.g., (
C.43
)). ∎

C.1
Technical lemmas for Theorem
5

In this section, we present the statements and proofs of the technical lemmas used in the proof of Theorem
5
. All notation is defined as needed throughout, and Assumptions
1
and
2
are assumed to hold unless otherwise specified. For notational simplicity, we use the shorthand
\|\cdot\|
to denote the Frobenius norm for matrices.

Lemma C.2
.

For any quadratic
q\in Q
such that
\|q\|_{C^{2}}\leq 1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\right|\leq\frac{2\gamma_{n}}{\rho^{2}n}C_{\eta,2},\quad\text{a.s.}

(C.16)

where
C_{\eta,2}
denotes the upper bound on the absolute value of the second derivative of
\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)
. In addition, there is a constant
C=C(c,\gamma,\rho,M)>0
, such that, for any
y\in[1,n]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{1\leq k\leq{(n\wedge\tau)}}\,|\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)|\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}y,

(C.17)

with probability at least
1-e^{-y}.

Proof.

Recall the definition in (
C.8
)

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)

\displaystyle=2c\sqrt{{d}}{\sigma}_{k}\langle{b_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}2c\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}{\sigma}_{k}n\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{g}_{k}^{\top}\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})b_{k}

\displaystyle+2dc^{2}\sigma_{k}^{2}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits((b_{k})^{\otimes 2}\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}))-2dc^{2}{\sigma}_{k}^{2}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})).

Then, for any
k\leq\tau,
we rewrite the martingale as a combination of the following three terms

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)=\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\langle{b_{k},A_{k,1}+A_{k,2}}\rangle+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(b_{k}^{\otimes 2}-I_{d}\right),

(C.18)

where we introduced the shorthands

A_{k,1}:=2cn^{3/2}\sqrt{{d}}{\sigma}_{k}\nabla q(u_{k}),\;A_{k,2}:=cn\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}{\sigma}_{k}n\bar{\eta}_{k}\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})\bar{g}_{k},\;C_{k}:=2dn^{2}c^{2}{\sigma}_{k}^{2}\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}).

(C.19)

We will separately bound the contribution of each term in terms of its Orlicz norm. Let us start with the second term, and
consider
q\in Q
. This is a quadratic function of the iterates; its Hessian, therefore, does not depend on the iterates and explicitly on
k.
In addition, we have that
\sup_{z\in\Omega}\|R(z;\Sigma)\|_{\text{op}}\leq 2
. Since
b_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and independent, using the Hanson-Wright inequality (Theorem 6.2.1 in
Vershynin (2018)
)
we have that, for some
c>0
and
K=\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\|b_{k+1,i}\|_{\psi_{2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left((b_{k}^{\otimes 2}-I_{d}\right)C_{k})\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-c\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(\frac{t^{2}n^{4}}{K^{4}\|C_{k}\|^{2}},\frac{tn^{2}}{K^{2}\|C_{k}\|_{\text{op}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-c^{\prime}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(\frac{t^{2}n^{4}}{d},{tn^{2}}\right)\right),

for some
c^{\prime}>0
independent of
k,d,n.
To justify the last passage, note that, by the structure of the noise,

\sigma_{k}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\rho^{2}n^{2}}|\tilde{\eta}({k}/{n})^{2}-\tilde{\eta}((k+1)/n)^{2}|\leq\frac{2}{\rho^{2}n^{3}}\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits_{x\in[\frac{k}{n},\frac{k+1}{n}]}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(x)\right|\leq\frac{2}{\rho^{2}n^{3}}C_{\eta,1},

as
\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(x)\right|\leq C_{\eta,1}
for some
C_{\eta,1}
due to Assumption
2
. Using the above bound, we obtain that
\|C_{k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2dn^{2}c^{2}\sigma_{k}^{2}\|q\|_{C^{2}}\leq{8}c^{2}/\rho^{2}C_{\eta,1},
and similarly
\|C_{k}\|\leq 8\sqrt{d}c^{2}/\rho^{2}C_{\eta,1}.

We, therefore, have that

\|\frac{1}{n^{2}}\langle{b_{k}^{\otimes 2}-I_{d},C_{k}}\rangle\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq Cn^{-1},
for some constant
C(\rho,c,C_{\eta,1})>0.

For the first term, by Eq. (89) in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, the norm of
q
is bounded for the stopped process
u_{k}^{\tau}
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\nabla q(u)\|\leq\|\nabla^{2}q\|_{\text{op}}\|u\|+\|\nabla q(0)\|\leq\|q\|_{C^{2}}(1+\|u\|)\leq C(1+M).

(C.20)

Hence, we have that
\|A_{k,1}\|\leq 2n^{3/2}c\sqrt{n}\sigma_{k}C(1+M)\leq 4\sqrt{d}c\sqrt{C_{\eta,1}/\gamma_{n}}C(1+M)/\rho
, which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{{n^{3/2}}}\langle{b_{k},A_{k,1}}\rangle\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\frac{C}{n},

(C.21)

with
C=C(M,\rho,\gamma_{n},C_{\eta,1},c)>0.

Then,
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{{n}^{3/2}}\langle{b_{k},A_{k,2}}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}cn\sqrt{\gamma_{n}}{\sigma}_{k}\tilde{\eta}(k/n)\left|\langle{b_{k},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})g_{k}}\rangle\right|

(C.22)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2c\sqrt{C_{\eta,1}}}{n\sqrt{\gamma_{n}}\rho}\left|\langle{b_{k},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})x_{k+1}}\rangle(\langle{x_{k+1},u_{k}}\rangle+z_{k+1})\right|.

Due to (
C.20
) and Assumptions
1
and
2
, we have
\|\langle{b_{k},\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})x_{k+1}}\rangle\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq C\|b_{k}\|_{\psi_{2}}\|x_{k+1}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C
for some
C>0
that depends on
\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
Finally, we note that
\|\langle{x_{k+1},u_{k}}\rangle\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C(1+M)
, and
\|z_{k}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C\zeta
for any
k\leq\tau
.
Combining the above, we obtain that there is a constant
C=C(c,C_{\eta,1},\rho,\gamma_{n},M,\zeta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\phi_{k,1}:=\inf\{t>0:\,\mathbb{E}[\exp(|\Delta\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)|/t)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]\leq 2\}\leq Cn^{-1}.

(C.23)

We then apply Lemma 5 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
for some absolute constant
C>0
for all
t>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{1\leq k\leq n\wedge\tau}|\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\text{Noise}}(q)-\mathbb{E}\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\text{Noise}}(q)|\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\{\frac{t}{C\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits_{k\in[n]}\phi_{k,1}},\frac{t^{2}}{C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i,1}^{2}}\}\right)

(C.24)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-Cn\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\{t,t^{2}\}\right).

As we assume that
n
is proportional to
d
and noting that
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\text{Noise}}(q)=0
by definition, we then have that, for any
y\in[1,n]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{1\leq k\leq{(n\wedge\tau})}\,|\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)|\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}y,

(C.25)

with probability at least
1-e^{-y}
for any
y\in[1,n].

Next, we bound the error due to the discretization:

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Noise}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq{2d}c^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\left|\sigma_{k}^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{3}}\tilde{\sigma}(k/n)^{2}\right|\cdot\left|\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2d^{2}\|q\|_{C^{2}}}{n^{2}\rho^{2}}c^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\left|\tilde{\eta}(k/n)^{2}-\tilde{\eta}(({k+1})/n)^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\sigma}(k/n)^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\gamma_{n}^{2}c^{2}\|q\|_{C^{2}}}{\rho^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor tn\rfloor}\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits_{x\in(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k+1}{n})}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}\tilde{\eta}(x)^{2}\right|,

where we use the definition of the noise function as the derivative of the learning rate
\rho^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(x)^{2}=-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(x)
at any point
x\in[0,1).
Then, as
|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(x)|\leq C_{\eta,2}
for some constant
C_{\eta,2}
, the desired result follows.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\textup{H-DP-GD},\tau}
the H-DP-GD martingale in which the stopping time is imposed. There is a constant
C=C(c,C_{\eta,1},\gamma_{n},\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}},M)>0
, such that for any quadratic
q\in Q
with
\|q\|_{C^{2}}\leq 1,
and for any
y\in[1,n]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t<{1}}\,|\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\textup{H-DP-GD},\tau}(q)|\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}y,

(C.26)

with probability at least
1-e^{-y}.

Proof.

To bound the martingale error from H-DP-GD under some general statistic
q\in Q
, differently from the argument to obtain Eq. (72) in Lemma 2 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
, we need to control the additional term due to the additive noise in Algorithm
1
.

Using Itô’s formula for any quadratic function
q
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}q(V_{t})=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\nabla\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})^{\top}\nabla q(V_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q\right)\mathrm{d}t

(C.27)

\displaystyle+2\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\nabla^{2}q\right)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}}

\displaystyle:=\langle{\nabla q(V_{t}),\sqrt{\frac{2\bar{\eta}(t)^{2}\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\Sigma}{n}+4\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(t)^{2}I_{d}}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}}\rangle,

with
B_{t}
being a standard
d
dimensional Brownian motion.
The quadratic variation of the martingale is then bounded a.s.

\langle{\mathcal{M}(q)}\rangle_{t}\leq\frac{Ct}{n}+\frac{d}{n^{2}}(4c^{2}\bar{\eta}^{2}\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}(1+M)^{2}),

for some constant
C=C(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}},M,C_{\eta,1},c,\gamma_{n})>0,

where we used Assumption
2
which gives
|\rho^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(t)|=|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)|\leq C_{\eta,1}
.
The claim is then proved by an application of Gaussian concentration inequalities as in Section B.6 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

For any
r\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-r^{2}/2}
, we jointly have

\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\|V_{t}\|^{2}\leq\|V_{0}\|^{2}e^{C(1+d^{-1/2}r)},\qquad\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\|u_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\|^{2}\leq\|u_{0}\|^{2}e^{C(1+d^{-1/2}r)}.

(C.28)

Proof.

The proof follows a path similar to the one of Lemma 4 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
. In particular, consider the function
\varphi(V_{t})=\log(1+\|V_{t}\|^{2}).
Then, by application of Itô’s Lemma to (
4.14
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\varphi(V_{t})=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\frac{\mu_{c}(\Theta_{t})}{1+\|V_{t}\|^{2}}\nabla\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})^{\top}V_{t}\mathrm{d}t

(C.29)

\displaystyle+{2}\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})}{(1+\|V_{t}\|^{2})^{2}}\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma(I_{d}(1+\|V_{t}\|^{2})-V_{t}\otimes V_{t})\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2\frac{d}{n}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\frac{1}{(1+\|V_{t}\|^{2})^{2}}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}(\varphi),

with

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{(1+\|V_{t}\|^{2})}\langle{V_{t},\sqrt{\frac{2\bar{\eta}(t)^{2}\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\Sigma}{n}+4\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}(t)^{2}I_{d}}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}}\rangle.

(C.30)

The drift terms and the quadratic variation terms can be bounded by some
C(c,\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}},\zeta,\gamma_{n},M)
. The quadratic variation of the martingale term is bounded as

\langle{M}\rangle_{t}\leq\frac{Ct}{n}.
We then have, by Gaussian concentration,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq t<1}\varphi(V_{t})\geq C(1+r/\sqrt{n})\right)\leq e^{-r^{2}/2},

(C.31)

which proves the first part of the claim. The bound on
\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\|u_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\|^{2}
follows the same strategy as the argument following Lemma 4 in
Marshall et al. (2025)
.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

For any quadratic
q\in Q
such that
\|q\|_{C^{2}}\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\sup_{1\leq k\leq{(n\wedge\tau)}}\,|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Step}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{2}n,

(C.32)

with probability at least
1-e^{-c\log^{2}d}
.

Proof.

Recall that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Step}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/d)}{n}-\bar{\eta}_{k}\right)\mu_{c}(u_{k})\langle{\Sigma u_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle

(C.33)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{\bar{\eta}_{k}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/n)^{2}}{2n^{2}}\right)\nu_{c}(u_{k})\mathcal{P}(u_{k})\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q(u_{k})),

and
\left\|u_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}<M
by the definition of
\tau
. Thus, this implies

\left|\mu_{c}(u_{k})\langle{\Sigma u_{k},\nabla q(u_{k})}\rangle\right|=O(1),\qquad\left|\nu_{c}(u_{k})\mathcal{P}(u_{k})\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q(u_{k}))\right|=O(d).

(C.34)

First, notice that

\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/d)}{n}-\bar{\eta}_{k}=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(\frac{\tilde{\eta}(k/d)}{n},\frac{2}{d}\right)-\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits\left(\frac{\tilde{\eta}(k/d)}{n},\frac{2}{\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right),

(C.35)

which implies

\left|\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/d)}{n}-\bar{\eta}_{k}\right|\leq 2\left|\frac{1}{d}-\frac{1}{\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right|=\frac{2\left|\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}-d\right|}{d\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

(C.36)

Similarly, it can be shown that

\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{\bar{\eta}(k/d)^{2}}{n^{2}}-\bar{\eta}_{k}^{2}\right|\leq\frac{2\left|\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}-d\right|\left(\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}+d\right)}{d^{2}\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{4}}.

(C.37)

Hanson-Wright inequality gives

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\left\|x_{k}\right\|_{2}^{2}-d\right|>\sqrt{d}\log d\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-c_{1}\log^{2}d\right),

(C.38)

which is sufficient to show that, with probability
1-2\exp\left(-c_{1}\log^{2}d\right)
,

\left|\mathbb{E}[\Delta\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\textup{Step}}(q)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\right|=O\left(\frac{\log d}{d^{3/2}}\right),

(C.39)

which yields the thesis after performing a union bound over
k\in[n]
.
∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
1

Consider (
C.27
), which reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}q(V_{t})=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\nabla\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})^{\top}\nabla q(V_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma\nabla^{2}q\right)\mathrm{d}t

(C.40)

\displaystyle+2\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\nabla^{2}q\right)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}}(q),

where we recall the notation
V_{t}=\Theta_{t}-\theta^{*}
, and
q(V_{t})=V_{t}^{\top}(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}V_{t}/2
, and where in Lemma
C.3
we showed that
\sup_{0\leq t<{1}}\,|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\textup{H-DP-GD}}(q)\mathrm{d}\tau|\leq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}y
with probability at least
1-e^{-y}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}q(V_{t})=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\Theta_{t})V_{t}^{\top}\Sigma(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}V_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\frac{1}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}t

(C.41)

\displaystyle+2\frac{d}{n^{2}}c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left((\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}}(q),

which, using the identity
\Sigma(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}=I+z(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}
, can be written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}q(V_{t})=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\left(\left\|V_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}+zq(V_{t})\right)\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\Theta_{t})\mathcal{P}(\Theta_{t})\gamma_{n}\frac{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\right)}{d}\mathrm{d}t

(C.42)

\displaystyle+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma_{n}^{2}\frac{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left((\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\right)}{d}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{t}^{\text{H-DP-GD}}(q).

Notice that

q(V_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\langle{V_{t},\omega_{i}}\rangle^{2}}{\lambda_{i}-z},\qquad\left\|V_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{i}{\pi}\oint_{\Omega}q(V_{t}),\qquad\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{t})=\frac{i}{2\pi}\oint_{\Omega}zq(V_{t}),

(C.43)

where the last two equations follow from Cauchy integral formula. Then, we can define the following (deterministic) integro-differential equation after removing the martingale term from (
C.27
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{q}(z,t)=

\displaystyle-\bar{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}\left(\frac{i}{2\pi}\oint_{\Omega}z\hat{q}(z,t)\right)\left(\frac{i}{\pi}\oint_{\Omega}\hat{q}(z,t)+z\hat{q}(z,t)\right)\mathrm{d}t

(C.44)

\displaystyle+\bar{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}\left(\frac{i}{2\pi}\oint_{\Omega}z\hat{q}(z,t)\right)\left(\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}+\frac{i}{2\pi}\oint_{\Omega}z\hat{q}(z,t)\right)\gamma_{n}\frac{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\right)}{d}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma_{n}^{2}\frac{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left((\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\right)}{d}\mathrm{d}t,

where all differentials are taken with respect to time. (
C.44
) is defined for
z\in\Omega
,
t\in[0,1]
, and is such that
\hat{q}(z,0)=q(V_{0})={\theta^{*}}^{\top}(\Sigma-zI)^{-1}\theta^{*}/2
.

Using the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5
based on Gronwall’s inequality, relying on
\zeta>0
which makes both
\mu_{c}(\theta)
and
\nu_{c}(\theta)\mathcal{P}(\theta)
Lipschitz with respect to
\mathcal{R}(\theta)
with constant-order Lipschitz constant, and since the length of
\Omega
is bounded, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left|\mathcal{R}(\Theta_{t})-R(t)\right|=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

(C.45)

with overwhelming probability, where we have introduce the shorthand
R(t)=\frac{i}{4\pi}\oint_{\Omega}z\hat{q}(z,t)
.

Then, due to Lemma 4.1 in
Collins-Woodfin et al. (2024a)
, we have that the unique solution of (
C.44
) is

\hat{q}(z,t)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{D_{i}(t)}{\lambda_{i}-z},

(C.46)

where
D_{i}(t)
is defined according to (
4.7
). Multiplying both sides by
z
, integrating it over
\Omega
and using Cauchy formula, together with Theorem
5
, gives the first desired result, i.e.,

\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{\lfloor tn\rfloor})-R(t)\right|=O\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

(C.47)

with overwhelming probability.

To prove the same inequality in expectation, define the event
\mathcal{E}
such that

\Delta:=\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{\lfloor tn\rfloor})-R(t)\right|\geq C_{1}\frac{\log^{2}n}{\sqrt{n}}.

(C.48)

By (
C.47
), there exists a constant
C_{1}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\leq 2e^{-c_{1}\log^{2}d}
. Then

\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\theta_{\lfloor tn\rfloor})\right]-R(t)\right|\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{\lfloor tn\rfloor})-R(t)\right|\right]\leq C_{1}\frac{\log^{2}n}{\sqrt{n}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\right].

(C.49)

Notice that

\Delta\leq\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{\lfloor tn\rfloor})\right|+C_{2}\leq C_{3}\left(1+\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left\|u_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right),

(C.50)

since
R(t)
exists in
t\in[0,1]
and does not depend on
d
and
n
, and since
\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{\textup{op}}=O(1)
. Thus, Cauchy-Schwartz inequality yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\right]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta^{2}\right]\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\leq 2C_{4}e^{-c_{1}\log^{2}d}\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left\|u_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right\|_{2}^{4}\right]\right).

(C.51)

By Lemma
C.4
, we have that for any
r\geq 0
,
Z=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left\|u_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|u_{0}\|^{2}e^{C_{4}(1+d^{-1/2}r)}
with probability at least
1-2e^{-r^{2}/2}
. This bound implies that
2\log Z
has a dimension free sub-Gaussian tail, which in turn implies that

\mathbb{E}[e^{2\log Z}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,1)}\left\|u_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right\|_{2}^{4}\right]=O(1)
, giving

\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\right]^{2}\leq C_{5}e^{-c_{1}\log^{2}d}=O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

(C.52)

Merging in (
C.49
) gives the desired result. ∎

C.3
Proof of Proposition
4.4

Due to the update rule in Algorithm
1
, we have

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n})

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n}-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}

(C.53)

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}+2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}+2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle-2\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}^{\top}\Sigma\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right)-4\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}^{\top}\Sigma C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}.

By Assumptions
1
and
2
, and due to the definition of
\bar{g}_{n}
, we have

\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{\textup{op}}^{1/2}\left|\bar{\eta}_{n}\right|\left\|\bar{g}_{n}\right\|_{2}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{n}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

(C.54)

Theorem 3.1.1 in
Vershynin (2018)
also guarantees that
\left\|b_{n}\right\|_{2}=O(\sqrt{d}+u)
with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-c_{1}u^{2}\right)
, for some absolute constant
c_{1}>0
. Thus, with this probability, we have

\left|4\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}^{\top}\Sigma C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}\right|=\left|4\bar{\eta}_{n}\bar{g}_{n}^{\top}\Sigma C_{\textup{clip}}\frac{\eta_{n}}{\rho}b_{n}\right|=O\left(\frac{1}{n}\,\sqrt{d}\,\sqrt{d}\,\frac{1}{n}(\sqrt{d}+u)\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{u}{d}\right),

(C.55)

which gives

\left|2\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n})-\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}+2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}\Sigma^{1/2}b_{n}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right|=O\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})}}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{u}{d}\right).

(C.56)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}+2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}\Sigma^{1/2}b_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(C.57)

\displaystyle\qquad+4C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}^{\top}\Sigma\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right).

However, since
b_{n}
is a standard Gaussian vector, we have that, with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-c_{2}u^{2}\right)
,

\displaystyle\left|4C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}b_{n}^{\top}\Sigma\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\textup{op}}\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}u

(C.58)

\displaystyle=4c\sqrt{d}\frac{\tilde{\eta}(1)}{\rho n}\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\textup{op}}\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}u

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}u}{n}\right)\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\theta_{n-1}-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})}u}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

Then, an application of the Hanson-Wright inequality (see Theorem 6.2.1 in
Vershynin (2018)
) yields

\left|\left\|2C_{\textup{clip}}\sigma_{n}\Sigma^{1/2}b_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}-4c^{2}d\frac{\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)}{\rho^{2}n^{2}}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma)\right|\leq 4c^{2}d\frac{\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)}{\rho^{2}n^{2}}\|\Sigma\|u=O\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{d}}\right),

(C.59)

with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-c_{3}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(\sqrt{d}u,u^{2})\right)
.

Putting everything together gives

\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n})-\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})-2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma_{n}^{2}/\rho^{2}\right|=O\left(\frac{\left(1+\sqrt{\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})}\right)(u+1)}{\sqrt{d}}\right),

(C.60)

with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-c_{4}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(\sqrt{d}u,u^{2})\right)
.

Setting
u=\log d
, we have that

\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n})-\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})-2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma_{n}^{2}/\rho^{2}\right|=O\left(\frac{\log d}{\sqrt{d}}\right),

(C.61)

with overwhelming probability, where we used
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma)=d
and Theorem
1
, which implies
\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})=O(1)
with overwhelming probability. Then, the first part of Theorem
1
and the triangle inequality concludes the first part of the proof.

For the result in expectation, notice that we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n})\right]-R(1)-2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma_{n}^{2}/\rho^{2}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n})-\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})-2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma_{n}^{2}/\rho^{2}\right|\right]

(C.62)

\displaystyle+\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})\right]-R(1)\right|.

The second term on the RHS is
O(\log^{2}n/\sqrt{n})
because of the second part of the statement of Theorem
1
, and due to continuity of
R(t)
in
t=1
. The argument of the first term of the RHS is the product of two random variables. The first one is such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(1+\sqrt{\mathcal{R}(\theta_{n-1})}\right)^{2}\right]\leq 2+2\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\theta_{-1})\right]=O(1),

(C.63)

due to the second part of the statement of Theorem
1
. The second one, due to (
C.60
), is a random variable
Z
such that
Z\leq(1+u)/\sqrt{d}
with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-c_{4}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(\sqrt{d}u,u^{2})\right)
. Thus,
\sqrt{d}Z
is sub-exponential with norm smaller than a constant, which implies

\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{2}\right]=O\left(\frac{1}{d}\right).

(C.64)

Plugging (
C.63
) and (
C.64
) in (
C.62
), and using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, gives the desired result. ∎

Appendix D
Proofs for Section
5

In this section, all asymptotic notations are with respect to the limit
\gamma\to 0
.

Proposition D.1
.

Define
\overline{R}(t),\underline{R}(t):[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
as the unique solutions of the following ODEs

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{R}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\overline{R})\overline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\underline{R}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\underline{R})\underline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

where
\overline{R}(0)=\underline{R}(0)=R(0)=\|\Sigma^{1/2}\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}/2
. Then, for every
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\underline{R}(t)\leq R(t)\leq\overline{R}(t),

(D.2)

where
R(t)
is defined in Definition
4.2
after taking
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma
.

Proof.

Until the end of the proof, we will define more auxiliary ODEs, such that the RHS is uniformly Lipschitz in the dependent variable at all times
t\in[0,1]
. Then, by the extension of the Picard–Lindelöf theorem (see Corollary 2.6 in
Teschl (2012)
),
we have that their solutions exist and are unique, and therefore also have continuous derivatives.
Then, defining

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{D}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(R(t))\overline{D}_{i}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{i}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(R(t))(R(t)+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\underline{D}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(R(t))\underline{D}_{i}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{i}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(R(t))(R(t)+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

by standard ODE comparison arguments (see Theorem 1.3 in
Teschl (2012)
)
we have that

\overline{D}_{i}(t)\geq D_{i}(t)\geq\underline{D}_{i}(t)
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Then, averaging over
i
(weighting by
\lambda_{i}
) the equations in (
D.3
) and (
4.7
), and defining
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\overline{D}_{i}(t)
and
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\underline{D}_{i}(t)
, we get
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\geq R(t)\geq\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, with

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(R(t))\overline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma^{2})}{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(R(t))(R(t)+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(R(t))\underline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma^{2})}{d}\,\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(R(t))(R(t)+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Furthermore, for a fixed value of
c
, we have that the functions
\mu_{c}(R)
and
\nu_{c}(R)(R+\zeta^{2}/2)
are respectively monotonically decreasing and increasing with respect to
R
(see Lemma
B.1
). Then, since by Jensen inequality we have that
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma^{2})\geq\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma)=d
(where the last step holds due to Assumption
1
), and since we also have
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma^{2})\geq\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma)=\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}d
,
by defining

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{R}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\overline{R})\overline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\underline{R}

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\underline{R})\underline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

again due to Theorem 1.3 in
Teschl (2012)
we get that
\overline{R}(t)\geq\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\geq R(t)\geq\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\geq\underline{R}(t)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, which gives the desired result.
∎

D.1
Proofs on output perturbation and constant private noise

All the ODEs defined in this (and the following) section will be such that their RHS is uniformly Lipschitz in the dependent variable at all times
t\in[0,1]
, which in turn guarantees they have a unique solution. Furthermore, the RHSs will also be uniformly Lipschitz with respect to the variable
t
due to Assumption
2
. Thus, if
R(0)=R^{\prime}(0)
, and

\mathrm{d}R=f(t,R),\qquad\mathrm{d}R^{\prime}=f^{\prime}(t,R^{\prime}),

(D.6)

with

f^{\prime}(t,R^{\prime}(t))\geq f(t,R^{\prime}(t)),

(D.7)

for all
t\in[0,1]
, we have that

R^{\prime}(t)\geq R(t)\,\text{ for all }\,t\in[0,1].

(D.8)

The same statement holds also for the opposite inequality, and will be used extensively to bound the solutions of
R(t)
for different schedules. This is a direct application of Theorem 1.3 in
Teschl (2012)
.

To ease the presentation, we introduce the notation
v=\tilde{\eta}(0)
. We will provide the proof separately for
\alpha=0
and
\alpha=1/2
, and the proof for
\alpha\geq 1
in the next section. We will also denote the test risk at initialization
R(0)=\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}/2
, and all asymptotic notations will be with respect to the limit
\gamma\to 0
.

The proof of Proposition
5.1
is given separately for the case
\alpha=0
and
\alpha=1/2
, in the general case where
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}
and
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
are allowed to depend on
\gamma
. The results are stated separately in Propositions
D.3
and
D.4
, and Proposition
5.1
is a direct consequence of them. At the end of this section we will discuss the sub-optimality of
c=\omega(1)
, to prove (
5.12
).

Lemma D.2
.

Let
\beta<1
and
E_{\beta}(x):\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be the exponential integral function defined as

E_{\beta}(x):=\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{e^{-xt}}{t^{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(D.9)

Then, we have that

\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}x^{1-\beta}E_{\beta}(x)=\Gamma(1-\beta),

(D.10)

where
\Gamma(\cdot)
denotes the Euler Gamma function

\Gamma(s):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(D.11)

Furthermore, we have that, for all
x\geq 0
,

E_{\beta}(x)\leq\Gamma(1-\beta)x^{-1+\beta}.

(D.12)

Finally, if either
\beta\geq 0
or
x\geq-2\beta
, we also have that

E_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{2e^{-x}}{x}.

(D.13)

Proof.

The change of variable
u=xt
in the definition of
E_{\beta}(x)
yields

\displaystyle E_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=x^{\beta-1}\int_{x}^{+\infty}\frac{e^{-u}}{u^{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}u

(D.14)

\displaystyle=x^{\beta-1}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-u}u^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}u-\int_{0}^{x}e^{-u}u^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}u\right)

\displaystyle=x^{\beta-1}\left(\Gamma(1-\beta)-\int_{0}^{x}e^{-u}u^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}u\right).

Then, (
D.10
) and (
D.12
) readily follow from the fact that the last term in the equation above is bounded by

0\leq\int_{0}^{x}e^{-u}u^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq\int_{0}^{x}u^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}u=\frac{x^{1-\beta}}{1-\beta}.

(D.15)

For the upper bound, denoting with

\Gamma(s,x):=\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t

(D.16)

the upper incomplete Euler gamma function, (
D.14
) allows us to write

E_{\beta}(x)=\frac{1}{x^{1-\beta}}\Gamma(1-\beta,x).

(D.17)

Via an integration by parts, we have

\Gamma(1-\beta,x)=\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t=e^{-x}x^{-\beta}-\beta\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\beta-1}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(D.18)

If
\beta\geq 0
, we have
\Gamma(1-\beta,x)\leq e^{-x}x^{-\beta}
, which together with (
D.17
) gives (
D.13
). If
\beta<0
, the second term in the equation above is positive, and since
t\geq x
, it can be upper bounded as

-\beta\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\beta-1}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq-\frac{\beta}{x}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t=-\frac{\beta}{x}\,\Gamma(1-\beta,x),

(D.19)

which, if plugged in (
D.18
), for
x\geq-2\beta
gives

\Gamma(1-\beta,x)\leq\frac{e^{-x}x^{-\beta}}{1+\beta/x}\leq 2e^{-x}x^{-\beta},

(D.20)

and the thesis again follows from (
D.17
).
∎

\alpha=0
: output perturbation.

Recall that in the setting
\alpha=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{R}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}vc\frac{\mu_{c}(\overline{R})}{c}\overline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}(vc)^{2}\frac{\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t,

(D.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\underline{R}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}vc\frac{\mu_{c}(\underline{R})}{c}\underline{R}\mathrm{d}t+(vc)^{2}\frac{\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t.

Importantly, recall that the risk
\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})
in this setting is not well approximated by
R(1)
, due to Proposition
4.4
.

Proposition D.3
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and let
\theta^{p}
be the solution obtained with Algorithm
1
, with the schedule defined in (
5.1
) for
\alpha=0
, in the setting
\gamma=d/n\to 0
. Furthermore, assume

\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\ln(1/\gamma)\gamma=o(1).

(D.22)

Then, by setting

c=O(1),\qquad vc=\frac{C\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}},\qquad v\leq 2/\gamma,

(D.23)

for a large enough constant
C
which does not depend on
\gamma,\rho,\Sigma
, we have that, with overwhelming probability,

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=O\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\right).

(D.24)

Furthermore, suppose there exists
h>0
such that
\rho=\Omega\left(\gamma^{1-h}\right)
. Then, for any choice of the hyper-parameters
c
and
v
such that
v\leq 2/\gamma
, we have that

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}\right).

(D.25)

Proof.

Let us introduce the shorthands

\displaystyle\overline{f}(t,\overline{R})

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}vc\frac{\mu_{c}(\overline{R})}{c}\overline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}(vc)^{2}\frac{\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t,

(D.26)

\displaystyle\underline{f}(t,\underline{R})

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}vc\frac{\mu_{c}(\underline{R})}{c}\underline{R}\mathrm{d}t+(vc)^{2}\frac{\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t,

corresponding to the RHSs of the ODEs of interest. Then, consider the auxiliary ODEs

\mathrm{d}\overline{R}^{\prime}=-\overline{a}\overline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}\mathrm{d}t=:\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime}=-\underline{a}\,\underline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\underline{b}\mathrm{d}t=:\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\overline{a},\overline{b},\underline{a},\underline{b}>0,

(D.27)

with initial conditions
\overline{R}^{\prime}(0)=\overline{R}(0)=R(0)=\underline{R}(0)=\underline{R}^{\prime}(0)
.

Notice that
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)
admits the closed form solution

\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\left(R(0)-\overline{b}/\overline{a}\right)e^{-\overline{a}t}+\overline{b}/\overline{a}.

(D.28)

Similarly, we have that

\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\left(R(0)-\underline{b}/\underline{a}\right)e^{-\underline{a}t}+\underline{b}/\underline{a}.

(D.29)

Since
c=O(1)
, by Lemma
B.2
, we have that

\frac{\underline{c}_{\mu}(c,\zeta)}{\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}}<\frac{\mu_{c}(R)}{c}<\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\left(R+\zeta^{2}/2\right)}},\qquad\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)<\frac{2\nu_{c}(R)\left(R+\zeta^{2}/2\right)}{c^{2}}<1.

(D.30)

Then, let us set

\overline{a}=2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}vc\frac{\underline{c}_{\mu}(c,\zeta)}{\sqrt{R(0)+1+\zeta^{2}}},\quad\overline{b}=\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\frac{v^{2}c^{2}\gamma}{2},\quad\underline{a}=2vc\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\sqrt{\pi\zeta^{2}/2}},\quad\underline{b}=\frac{v^{2}c^{2}\gamma\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)}{2}.

(D.31)

As
cv=C\ln(1/\gamma)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
, the choice in (
D.31
) ensures that
\underline{b}/\underline{a}\leq 1
and
\overline{b}/\overline{a}\leq 1
as long as
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}\ln(1/\gamma)\gamma=o(1)
, which in turn guarantees that

\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\in[0,R(0)+1],\qquad\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\in[0,R(0)+1].

(D.32)

Thus, (
D.31
) guarantees

\overline{f}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime}(t))<\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)),\qquad\underline{f}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime}(t))>\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)),\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in[0,1].

(D.33)

Thus, by (
D.8
), we have

\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)<\underline{R}(t),\qquad\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)>\overline{R}(t)\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in(0,1].

(D.34)

In particular, plugging
vc=C\ln(1/\gamma)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
and (
D.31
) in (
D.28
), we have that

\overline{R}(1)<\overline{R}^{\prime}(1)<e^{-\overline{a}}+\frac{\overline{b}}{\overline{a}}=O\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\ln(1/\gamma)\gamma\right),

(D.35)

as long as
C
is chosen to be large enough. Then, the upper bounds follows from Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
,
as the risk at the last iterate roughly increases by
2c^{2}v^{2}\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}=O(\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}(1/\gamma)/(\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}))
.

For the lower bound, let us first suppose
c\leq 1
, which implies that (
D.30
) holds with
\underline{c}_{\mu}(c,\zeta)
and
\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)
lower bounded by constants independent of
\gamma
. Pick
\underline{a},\underline{b}
as in (
D.31
).

First, let us consider the case
\underline{a}\leq 1
. We have that (
D.34
) gives

\underline{R}(1)>\underline{R}^{\prime}(1)=R(0)e^{-\underline{a}}+\frac{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}-\frac{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}e^{-\underline{a}}>R(0)e^{-\underline{a}}\geq R(0)/e=\Omega(1).

(D.36)

In the case
\underline{a}>1
, (
D.34
) gives

\underline{R}(1)>\underline{R}^{\prime}(1)=R(0)e^{-\underline{a}}+\frac{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}-\frac{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}e^{-\underline{a}}>R(0)e^{-\underline{a}}+\frac{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}(1-e^{-1})=R(0)\left(e^{-vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}+\frac{vcb\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}\right),

(D.37)

where
a,b
are positive constants which do not depend on
\gamma,v,c
and the spectrum of
\Sigma
.
Then, let us consider the following quantity

\underline{\mathcal{R}}:=R(0)\left(e^{-vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}+\frac{vcb\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}\right)+2v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}.

(D.38)

We have that

\arg\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{vc}e^{-vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}+\frac{vcb\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}\ln\left(\frac{(\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a)^{2}}{b\gamma}\right),

(D.39)

which implies

\underline{\mathcal{R}}>R(0)\left(e^{-vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}+\frac{vcb\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}\right).

(D.40)

Note that, assuming
\rho=\Omega(\gamma^{1-h})
, we have

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{vc\geq\frac{\ln(\rho^{2}/\gamma^{2})}{2a\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}}&e^{-vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}+v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\geq\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{vc\geq\frac{\ln(\rho^{2}/\gamma^{2})}{2a\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}}v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right),\\
\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{0\leq vc\leq\frac{\ln(\rho^{2}/\gamma^{2})}{2a\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}}&e^{-vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}a}+v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\geq\ \frac{\gamma}{\rho}=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right).\end{split}

(D.41)

Then, merging (
D.38
), (
D.40
) and (
D.41
) yields

\underline{\mathcal{R}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right).

(D.42)

The result in (
D.37
) together with Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
imply that, with overwhelming probability,

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right).

(D.43)

It remains to prove the lower bound for
c>1
. From (
B.1
), one readily has that

0<\mu_{c}(R)<1
. Note that

\nu_{c}(R)(R+\zeta^{2}/2)\geq\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits\left(\frac{\nu_{c}(R)(R+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}},\nu_{c}(R)\zeta^{2}/2\right),

(D.44)

which combined with (
B.17
) gives that
\nu_{c}(R)(R+\zeta^{2}/2)>b_{1}
, for a positive constant
b_{1}
independent of
\gamma,v,c
and the spectrum of
\Sigma
. Furthermore, (
B.15
) implies that
\nu_{c}(R)(R+\zeta^{2}/2)<c^{2}/2
. Thus, the solution of
\underline{R}
is lower bounded by that of the ODE below:

\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}v\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}\mathrm{d}t+v^{2}b_{1}\gamma\mathrm{d}t,

(D.45)

with initial condition
\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}(0)=R(0)
.
Thus, following the same steps we used to achieve (
D.42
), it can be shown that, as
c\geq 1
, we have, with overwhelming probability,

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right),

(D.46)

which gives the desired result.
∎

\alpha=1/2
: constant private noise.

In the setting
\alpha=1/2
, (
5.1
) gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{R}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}vc\sqrt{1-t}\frac{\mu_{c}(\overline{R})}{c}\overline{R}\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}(vc)^{2}(1-t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2(vc)^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\underline{R}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}vc\sqrt{1-t}\frac{\mu_{c}(\underline{R})}{c}\underline{R}\mathrm{d}t+(vc)^{2}(1-t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t+2(vc)^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{d}t.

Importantly, recall that the risk
\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})
in this setting is well approximated by
R(1)
, due to Proposition
4.4
, since
\tilde{\eta}(1)=0
.

Proposition D.4
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and let
\theta^{p}
be the solution obtained with Algorithm
1
, with the schedule defined in (
5.1
) for
\alpha=1/2
, in the setting
\gamma=d/n\to 0
. Furthermore, assume

\frac{\ln^{2}(1/\gamma)\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\left(\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}+\frac{\gamma}{\rho^{2}}\right)=o(1).

(D.48)

Then, by setting

c=O(1),\qquad vc=\frac{C\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}},\qquad v\leq 2/\gamma,

(D.49)

for a large enough constant
C
which does not depend on
\gamma,\rho,\Sigma
, we have that, with overwhelming probability,

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=O\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\gamma\ln^{2/3}(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{4/3}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\right).

(D.50)

Furthermore, suppose there exists
h>0
such that
\rho=\Omega\left(\gamma^{1-h}\right)
. Then, for any choice of the hyper-parameters
c
and
v
such that
v\leq 2/\gamma
, we have that

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln^{2/3}(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{4/3}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}\right).

(D.51)

Proof.

As before, let us introduce the shorthands

\displaystyle\overline{f}(t,\overline{R})
