\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}vc\sqrt{1-t}\frac{\mu_{c}(\overline{R})}{c}\overline{R}+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}(vc)^{2}(1-t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma+2(vc)^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}},

(D.52)

\displaystyle\underline{f}(t,\underline{R})

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}vc\sqrt{1-t}\frac{\mu_{c}(\underline{R})}{c}\underline{R}+(vc)^{2}(1-t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma+2(vc)^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}.

Then, consider the auxiliary ODEs

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\overline{R}^{\prime}&=-\overline{a}\sqrt{1-t}\overline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}_{1}(1-t)\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}_{2}\mathrm{d}t=:\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\overline{a},\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2}>0,\\
\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime}&=-\underline{a}\sqrt{1-t}\underline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\underline{b}_{1}(1-t)\mathrm{d}t+\underline{b}_{2}\mathrm{d}t=:\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\underline{a},\underline{b}_{1},\underline{b}_{2}>0,\end{split}

(D.53)

with initial conditions
\overline{R}^{\prime}(0)=\overline{R}(0)=R(0)=\underline{R}(0)=\underline{R}^{\prime}(0)
, which admit the closed-form solutions

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{R(0)}{3}&e^{-2\overline{a}(1-(1-t)^{3/2})/3}\biggl(3\,+\\
&-\frac{2}{R(0)}\overline{b}_{1}e^{2\overline{a}/3}\left(E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}}{3}\right)-(1-t)^{2}E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\right)\\
&-\frac{2}{R(0)}\overline{b}_{2}e^{2\overline{a}/3}\left(E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}}{3}\right)-(1-t)E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\right)\biggr),\end{split}

(D.54)

\begin{split}\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{R(0)}{3}&e^{-2\underline{a}(1-(1-t)^{3/2})/3}\biggl(3\,+\\
&-\frac{2}{R(0)}\underline{b}_{1}e^{2\underline{a}/3}\left(E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)-(1-t)^{2}E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\right)\\
&-\frac{2}{R(0)}\underline{b}_{2}e^{2\underline{a}/3}\left(E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)-(1-t)E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\right)\biggr),\end{split}

(D.55)

expressed in terms of the exponential integral functions
E_{-1/3}(\cdot)
,
E_{1/3}(\cdot)
defined in (
D.9
).

Note that, for
t\in[0,1]
,
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
. In fact, if this is not the case, by continuity of
\overline{R}^{\prime}
, there exists an interval
(t^{*},t^{*}+\delta)\subseteq[0,1]
s.t.
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
and
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)<0
for all
t\in(t^{*},t^{*}+\delta)
. However, if
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
, then the derivative of
\overline{R}^{\prime}
evaluated at
t^{*}
is
\geq b_{2}>0
by (
D.53
), which is a contradiction. A similar argument gives that, for
t\in[0,1]
,
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
.

Next, we upper bound
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)
as

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq R(0)&+\frac{2}{3}\overline{b}_{1}e^{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3}(1-t)^{2}E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\\
&+\frac{2}{3}\overline{b}_{2}e^{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3}(1-t)E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right).\end{split}

(D.56)

If
2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3\geq 2/3
, an application of (
D.13
) gives that

\begin{split}e^{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3}(1-t)^{2}&E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\overline{a}}\sqrt{1-t}\leq\frac{3}{\overline{a}},\\
e^{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3}(1-t)&E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\overline{a}}(1-t)^{-1/2}\leq\frac{3}{\overline{a}^{2/3}}.\end{split}

(D.57)

Instead, if
2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3<2/3
, by using (
D.12
) we have

\begin{split}e^{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3}(1-t)^{2}&E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\leq e^{2/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\left(\frac{2\overline{a}}{3}\right)^{-4/3},\\
e^{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}/3}(1-t)&E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}(1-t)^{3/2}}{3}\right)\leq e^{2/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\left(\frac{2\overline{a}}{3}\right)^{-2/3}.\end{split}

(D.58)

Thus, the following upper bound holds for
t\in[0,1]
:

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq R(0)&+\frac{2\overline{b}_{1}}{\overline{a}}+\frac{2\overline{b}_{2}}{\overline{a}^{2/3}}+e^{2/3}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{-1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\frac{\overline{b}_{1}}{\overline{a}^{4/3}}+e^{2/3}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\frac{\overline{b}_{2}}{\overline{a}^{2/3}}.\end{split}

(D.59)

Following the same passages, we have that, for
t\in[0,1]
,

\begin{split}\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq R(0)&+\frac{2\underline{b}_{1}}{\underline{a}}+\frac{2\underline{b}_{2}}{\underline{a}^{2/3}}+e^{2/3}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{-1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\frac{\underline{b}_{1}}{\underline{a}^{4/3}}+e^{2/3}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\frac{\underline{b}_{2}}{\underline{a}^{2/3}}.\end{split}

(D.60)

Let us now pick

\begin{split}\overline{a}&=2vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\frac{\underline{c}_{\mu}(c,\zeta)}{\sqrt{R(0)+1+\zeta^{2}/2}},\qquad\overline{b}_{1}=\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\frac{v^{2}c^{2}\gamma}{2},\qquad\overline{b}_{2}=4v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}},\\
\underline{a}&=2vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\zeta^{2}/2}},\qquad\underline{b}_{1}=\frac{v^{2}c^{2}\gamma\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)}{2},\qquad\underline{b}_{2}=v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}.\end{split}

(D.61)

Since
c=O(1)
, by Lemma
B.2
, we have that (
D.30
) holds.
As
cv=C\ln(1/\gamma)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
, the choice in (
D.61
) ensures that
0\leq\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq 1+R(0)
for
t\in[0,1]
, as long as we have
\overline{b}_{1}+\overline{b}_{2}=o(1)
, which holds due to (
D.48
).

Thus, (
D.61
) guarantees

\overline{f}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime}(t))<\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)),\qquad\underline{f}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime}(t))>\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)),\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in[0,1].

(D.62)

Note that
\overline{f}(t,R)
and
\underline{f}(t,R)
are continuous in both variables in the intervals
t\in[0,1]
and
R\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, they are also Lipschitz in
R
in these same intervals. Thus, (
D.8
) gives

\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)<\underline{R}(t),\qquad\overline{R}(t)<\overline{R}^{\prime}(t),\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in(0,1].

(D.63)

To prove the upper bound, due to Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
, it suffices to give an upper bound on
\overline{R}^{\prime}(1)
. To this aim, note that Lemma
D.2
yields

\begin{split}\lim_{x\to 0}&\,x^{2}\,E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2ax^{3/2}}{3}\right)=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{4/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)a^{-4/3},\\
\lim_{x\to 0}&\,x\,E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2ax^{3/2}}{3}\right)=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)a^{-2/3},\end{split}

(D.64)

where
\Gamma(\cdot)
denotes the Euler Gamma function (defined in (
D.11
)). Thus,

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(1)&=\frac{R(0)}{3}e^{-2\overline{a}/3}\biggl(3-\frac{2}{R(0)}\overline{b}_{1}e^{2\overline{a}/3}\left(E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}}{3}\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{4/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\overline{a}^{-4/3}\right)\\
&\hskip 60.00009pt-\frac{2}{R(0)}\overline{b}_{2}e^{2\overline{a}/3}\left(E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\overline{a}}{3}\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\overline{a}^{-2/3}\right)\biggr)\\
&\leq R(0)e^{-2\overline{a}/3}+\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\overline{b}_{1}\overline{a}^{-4/3}+\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{-1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\overline{b}_{2}\overline{a}^{-2/3},\end{split}

(D.65)

where in the last line we have used the non-negativity of the exponential integral functions.
For
C
sufficiently large, due to (
D.61
), the first term in the RHS is
o(\gamma)
(recall that
vc=C\ln(1/\gamma)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
). The other two terms are
O(\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\gamma\ln^{2/3}(1/\gamma)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2})
and
O(\gamma^{2}\ln^{4/3}(1/\gamma)/(\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2})
respectively. Note that

\overline{R}(1)<\overline{R}^{\prime}(1)
and
\tilde{\eta}(1)=0
. Thus, the desired result follows from Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
.

To prove the lower bound, due to Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
,
it suffices to show that the inequality in the thesis holds for
\underline{R}(1)
. Let us first suppose
c\leq 1
, which implies that (
D.30
) holds. Pick
\underline{a},\underline{b}_{1},\underline{b}_{2}
as in (
D.61
), and consider the ODE
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)
defined in (
D.53
) with the initial condition
\underline{R}^{\prime}(0)=R(0)
, which is a lower bound on
\underline{R}(t)
due to (
D.63
), i.e.,

\begin{split}\underline{R}(1)>\underline{R}^{\prime}(1)&=\frac{R(0)}{3}e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\biggl(3-\frac{2}{R(0)}\underline{b}_{1}e^{2\underline{a}/3}\left(E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{4/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\underline{a}^{-4/3}\right)\\
&\hskip 60.00009pt-\frac{2}{R(0)}\underline{b}_{2}e^{2\underline{a}/3}\left(E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\underline{a}^{-2/3}\right)\biggr).\end{split}

(D.66)

We consider two additional cases depending on the value of
\underline{a}
. If
\underline{a}\geq 2
, then (
D.13
) gives that

E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)\leq\frac{2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}}{\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}},\qquad E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)\leq\frac{2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}}{\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}},

(D.67)

which implies that

\begin{split}\underline{R}(1)\geq R(0)e^{-2\underline{a}/3}&+\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-4/3}-2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-1}\\
&+\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{-1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-2/3}-2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-1}.\end{split}

(D.68)

Note that

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-4/3}-2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-1}\geq\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-4/3}-2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-1}\geq\frac{1}{3}\underline{b}_{1}\underline{a}^{-4/3},\\
&\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{-1/3}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-2/3}-2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-1}\geq\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-2/3}-2e^{-2\underline{a}/3}\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-1}\geq\frac{1}{3}\underline{b}_{2}\underline{a}^{-2/3},\\
\end{split}

(D.69)

where the inequalities on the right hold for
\underline{a}\geq 2
. Thus,

\displaystyle\underline{R}(1)\geq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{R(0)}{2}e^{-avc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}+\frac{b_{1}}{3}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{-4/3}a^{-4/3}(vc)^{2/3}\gamma\right)

(D.70)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{R(0)}{2}e^{-avc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}+\frac{b_{2}}{3}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{-2/3}a^{-2/3}(vc)^{4/3}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right),

where
a,b_{1},b_{2}
are positive constants which do not depend on
\gamma,v,c,\rho
or the spectrum of
\Sigma
.

Denoting with
a^{\prime}=a\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}
,
\tau=vc
, we have that for a fixed
\beta\in\{2/3,4/3\}
, and for any
\delta=o(1)
,

\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{\tau\geq 0}e^{-\tau a^{\prime}}+\frac{\tau^{2-\beta}\delta}{(a^{\prime})^{\beta}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\delta\ln^{2-\beta}(1/\delta)}{(a^{\prime})^{2}}\right),

(D.71)

which follows from the following calculations:

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{\tau\geq\ln(1/\delta)/(2a^{\prime})}&e^{-\tau a^{\prime}}+\frac{\tau^{2-\beta}\delta}{(a^{\prime})^{\beta}}\geq\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{\tau\geq\ln(1/\delta)/(2a^{\prime})}\frac{\tau^{2-\beta}\delta}{(a^{\prime})^{\beta}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\delta\ln^{2-\beta}(1/\delta)}{(a^{\prime})^{2}}\right),\\
\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits_{0\leq\tau\leq\ln(1/\delta)/(2a^{\prime})}&e^{-\tau a^{\prime}}+\frac{\tau^{2-\beta}\delta}{(a^{\prime})^{\beta}}\geq\delta^{1/2}=\Omega(\delta\ln^{2-\beta}(1/\delta))=\Omega\left(\frac{\delta\ln^{2-\beta}(1/\delta)}{(a^{\prime})^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(D.72)

Then, since
\rho=\Omega(\gamma^{1-h})
, we can set
\delta=\gamma
and
\delta=\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}
to obtain

\underline{R}(1)=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln^{2/3}(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{4/3}(\rho^{2}/\gamma^{2})}{\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}\right),

(D.73)

which implies the desired result (due to Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
).

If
\underline{a}\leq 2
, then (
D.12
) gives that

E_{-1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)\leq\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)^{-4/3},\qquad E_{1/3}\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)\leq\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)\left(\frac{2\underline{a}}{3}\right)^{-2/3},

(D.74)

which implies that

\begin{split}\underline{R}(1)\geq R(0)e^{-2\underline{a}/3}=\Omega(1),\end{split}

(D.75)

thus again proving the desired claim (due to Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
).

Finally, for
c>1
, due to the same argument used to show (
D.44
), the solution of the original ODE is lower bounded by that of the ODE below:

\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}v\sqrt{1-t}\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}\mathrm{d}t+v^{2}b_{1}\gamma(1-t)\mathrm{d}t+2v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.76)

with initial condition
\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}(0)=R(0)
, where
b_{1}
is a positive constant independent of
\gamma,v,c,\rho
and the spectrum of
\Sigma
. Thus, following the same steps above with
\underline{a}=2v\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits},\underline{b}_{1}=v^{2}b_{1}\gamma,\underline{b}_{2}=2v^{2}c^{2}\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}
, one readily shows that

\underline{R}^{\prime\prime}(1)=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln^{2/3}(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\ln^{4/3}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right),

(D.77)

concluding the proof (due to Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
).
∎

Sub-optimality of
c=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
.

Note that, if
c>1
, the ODE in (
D.45
) maps to the one in (
D.29
), with
\underline{a},\underline{b}
defined as in (
D.31
) (except for absolute constants), with
vc\mapsto v
. This mapping also holds when defining (
D.38
), via
\rho\mapsto\rho/c
. Then, if we consider the further condition
\rho/c=\Omega(\gamma^{1-h})
, for some
h>0
, the lower bound in Proposition
5.1
becomes

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p}_{0})=\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits(1,c^{2})\gamma^{2}\ln^{2}\left(1/\gamma\right)}{\rho^{2}\lambda^{2}_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}\right).

(D.78)

The same argument holds also for the ODE in (
D.76
), providing analogous expression for
\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p}_{1/2})
. This quantifies the negative effects of using a large clipping constant
c=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
.

D.2
Proofs on decaying private noise:
\alpha\geq 1

In this section, we prove Proposition
5.2
. The result is stated in the general case where
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}
and
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
are allowed to depend on
\gamma
.

Proposition
5.2
(general version)
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and let
\theta^{p}_{\alpha}
be the solution obtained with Algorithm
1
, with
\tilde{\eta}(t)
given by (
5.1
) for
\alpha\geq 1
. Consider the setting

\gamma=\frac{d}{n}=o_{\gamma}(1),\qquad\frac{\ln^{2}(1/\gamma)\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\left(\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\alpha+\frac{\gamma}{\rho^{2}}\right)=o_{\gamma}(1),

(D.79)

and pick

c=O_{\gamma}(1),\qquad\tilde{\eta}(0)c=\frac{C\alpha\ln(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}},\qquad\tilde{\eta}(0)\leq\frac{2}{\gamma},

(D.80)

for a large enough constant
C
which does not depend on
\gamma,\rho,\Sigma,\alpha
. Then, we have that, with overwhelming probability,

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p}_{\alpha})=O_{\gamma}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\alpha\gamma\ln^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}(1/\gamma)+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\frac{\alpha^{2}\gamma^{2}\ln^{\frac{2}{1+\alpha}}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}}\right).

(D.81)

Proof.

Let
\overline{R}(t)
be defined as in (
D.1
), with the learning rate schedule in (
5.1
) for a generic
\alpha\geq 1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{R}

\displaystyle=-2vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\left(1-t\right)^{\alpha}\frac{\mu_{c}(\overline{R})}{c}\overline{R}\mathrm{d}t+(vc)^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\left(1-t\right)^{2\alpha}\frac{\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma\mathrm{d}t

(D.82)

\displaystyle+4(vc)^{2}\alpha\left(1-t\right)^{2\alpha-1}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{d}t.

The argument is similar to the one used to obtain Proposition
5.1
, so we only highlight differences. Using (
D.8
), we have that
\overline{R}(t)
is strictly bounded by the auxiliary ODEs

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\overline{R}^{\prime}&=-\overline{a}(1-t)^{\alpha}\overline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}_{1}(1-t)^{2\alpha}\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}_{2}(1-t)^{2\alpha-1}\mathrm{d}t=:\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\overline{a},\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2}>0,\\
\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime}&=-\underline{a}(1-t)^{\alpha}\underline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\underline{b}_{1}(1-t)^{2\alpha}\mathrm{d}t+\underline{b}_{2}(1-t)^{2\alpha-1}\mathrm{d}t=:\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\underline{a},\underline{b}_{1},\underline{b}_{2}>0,\end{split}

(D.83)

with initial conditions
\overline{R}^{\prime}(0)=\overline{R}(0)=R(0)
.

To establish a closed form solution for the ODEs in (
D.83
), we start by analyzing the ODE

\mathrm{d}\tilde{R}=-\overline{a}(1-t)^{\alpha}\tilde{R}\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}_{1}(1-t)^{2\alpha}\mathrm{d}t,

(D.84)

with initial condition
\tilde{R}(0)=R(0)
, which admits the closed form solution

\begin{split}\tilde{R}(t)=\frac{R(0)}{(1+\alpha)}&e^{-\frac{\overline{a}(1-(1-t)^{1+\alpha})}{1+\alpha}}\biggl(1+\alpha-\frac{\overline{b}_{1}}{R(0)}e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{\overline{b}_{1}}{R(0)}e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}(1-t)^{1+2\alpha}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)\biggr).\end{split}

(D.85)

Let

w(t)=\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)-\tilde{R}(t),

(D.86)

and note that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{R}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}t}-\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{R}}{\mathrm{d}t}

(D.87)

\displaystyle=-\overline{a}(1-t)^{\alpha}\overline{R}+\overline{a}(1-t)^{\alpha}\tilde{R}+\overline{b}_{2}(1-t)^{2\alpha-1}

\displaystyle=-\overline{a}(1-t)^{\alpha}w+\overline{b}_{2}(1-t)^{2\alpha-1}.

Thus, by using the initial condition
w(0)=\overline{R}^{\prime}(0)-\tilde{R}(0)=0
, we have

w(t)=\frac{\overline{b}_{2}}{1+\alpha}e^{\frac{\overline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}}\left((1-t)^{2\alpha}E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)-E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\right),

(D.88)

which implies that

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{R(0)}{(1+\alpha)}&e^{-\frac{\overline{a}(1-(1-t)^{1+\alpha})}{1+\alpha}}\biggl(1+\alpha-\frac{\overline{b}_{1}}{R(0)}e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{\overline{b}_{1}}{R(0)}e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}(1-t)^{1+2\alpha}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)\\
+\frac{\overline{b}_{2}}{R(0)}&e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}\left((1-t)^{2\alpha}E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)-E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\right)\biggr).\end{split}

(D.89)

Similarly, we have that

\begin{split}\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{(1+\alpha)}&e^{-\frac{\underline{a}(1-(1-t)^{1+\alpha})}{1+\alpha}}\biggl(R(0)(1+\alpha)-\underline{b}_{1}e^{\frac{\underline{a}}{1+\alpha}}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\underline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\underline{b}_{1}e^{\frac{\underline{a}}{1+\alpha}}(1-t)^{1+2\alpha}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\underline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)\\
+\underline{b}_{2}&e^{\frac{\underline{a}}{1+\alpha}}\left((1-t)^{2\alpha}E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\underline{a}(1-t)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)-E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\underline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\right)\biggr).\end{split}

(D.90)

For
t\in[0,1]
,
\overline{R}(t),\underline{R}(t)\geq 0
. Furthermore, the following upper bounds hold for
t\in[0,1]
:

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq R(0)+\frac{2\overline{b}_{1}}{\overline{a}}+\frac{\overline{b}_{2}}{\alpha}&+\overline{b}_{1}\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)^{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\Gamma\left(2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)\overline{a}^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\\
&+\overline{b}_{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}\right)(1+\alpha)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{1+\alpha}}\overline{a}^{-\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}}.\end{split}

(D.91)

Let us take

\overline{a}=C_{1}vc\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits},\qquad\overline{b}_{1}=C_{2}\gamma v^{2}c^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits},\qquad\overline{b}_{2}=C_{3}v^{2}c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\alpha,

(D.92)

with
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
constants independent from
\gamma,\rho,\alpha,v,c
or the spectrum of
\Sigma
. Then, we have that
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq 1+R(0)
for
t\in[0,1]
, as
\overline{a}=\Omega(\ln(1/\gamma))
and

\frac{\ln^{2}(1/\gamma)\gamma}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\left(\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\alpha+\frac{\gamma}{\rho^{2}}\right)=o(1).

(D.93)

As a result, we can apply the argument in (
D.8
) to obtain that

\overline{R}(1)\leq\overline{R}^{\prime}(1)=\tilde{R}(1)+w(1).

(D.94)

Note that Lemma
D.2
yields

\begin{split}\lim_{x\to 0}&x^{1+2\alpha}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}x^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\Gamma\left(2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)\overline{a}^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}.\end{split}

(D.95)

Thus,

\begin{split}\tilde{R}(1)=\frac{1}{(1+\alpha)}e^{-\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}\biggl(R(0)&(1+\alpha)-\overline{b}_{1}e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}E_{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\\
&+\overline{b}_{1}e^{\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\Gamma\left(2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)\overline{a}^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\biggr)\\
&\hskip-110.00017pt\leq R(0)e^{-\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}+\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)^{-1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\Gamma\left(2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)\overline{b}_{1}\overline{a}^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\\
&\hskip-110.00017pt\leq R(0)e^{-\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}+(1+\alpha)\overline{b}_{1}\overline{a}^{-2+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}},\end{split}

(D.96)

where in the second line we have used the non-negativity of the exponential integral function and in the third line we have used that
\Gamma\left(2-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\right)\leq\Gamma(2)=1
for all
\alpha\geq 1
. Next, we bound
w(1)
as

\begin{split}w(1)&=\frac{\overline{b}_{2}}{1+\alpha}\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}\right)(1+\alpha)^{\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\overline{a}^{-\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}}-E_{\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\left(\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}\right)\right)\\
&\leq\frac{\overline{b}_{2}}{1+\alpha}\Gamma\left(\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}\right)(1+\alpha)^{\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\overline{a}^{-\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\\
&\leq(1+\alpha)\overline{b}_{2}\overline{a}^{-\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}},\end{split}

(D.97)

where in the last line we have used that
\Gamma\left(\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}\right)\leq 1
for
\alpha\geq 1
.
By combining (
D.94
), (
D.96
) and (
D.97
), we conclude that

\begin{split}R(1)&\leq R(0)e^{-\frac{\overline{a}}{1+\alpha}}+(1+\alpha)^{1/3}\overline{b}_{1}\overline{a}^{-\frac{2\alpha+1}{1+\alpha}}+(1+\alpha)\overline{b}_{2}\overline{a}^{-\frac{2\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\\
&=O\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\alpha\gamma\ln^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}(1/\gamma)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\gamma^{2}\ln^{\frac{2}{1+\alpha}}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(D.98)

which gives the thesis, after applying Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
, since
\tilde{\eta}(1)=0
.
∎

Proof of (
5.17
).

By taking
\alpha=\ln\ln(1/\gamma)
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha\gamma\ln^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}(1/\gamma)+\frac{\alpha^{2}\gamma^{2}\ln^{\frac{2}{1+\alpha}}(1/\gamma)}{\rho^{2}}

(D.99)

\displaystyle=\gamma\ln\ln(1/\gamma)e^{\frac{\ln\ln(1/\gamma)}{1+\ln\ln(1/\gamma)}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}(\ln\ln(1/\gamma))^{2}e^{\frac{2\ln\ln(1/\gamma)}{1+\ln\ln(1/\gamma)}}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(\ln\ln(1/\gamma))e+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}(\ln\ln(1/\gamma))^{2}e^{2},

which readily gives (
5.17
), as
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits},\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}=\Theta_{\gamma}(1)
. ∎

D.3
Proofs on the harmonic schedule

In this section, we prove Theorem
2
, providing a more general result that allows for
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}
and
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
to depend on
\gamma
.

Lemma D.5
.

Consider the ODE

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\tilde{R}&=-a\tilde{\eta}(t)\tilde{R}\mathrm{d}t+b_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t-b_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{d}t,\qquad a,b_{1},b_{2}>0,\end{split}

(D.100)

with initial condition
\tilde{R}(0)
. Then,

\tilde{R}(t)=e^{-aF(t)}\left(\tilde{R}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{aF(s)}\bigl(b_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)-2b_{2}\tilde{\eta}(s)\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\right),

(D.101)

where
\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(t)
denotes the derivative of
\tilde{\eta}(t)
and
F(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\eta}(s)\mathrm{d}s
.

Proof.

Define

u(t):=\tilde{R}(t)+b_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t).

Then,

\mathrm{d}u(t)=-a\tilde{\eta}(t)u(t)\mathrm{d}t+b_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t+ab_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{3}(t)\mathrm{d}t,

which implies

\mathrm{d}\left(u(t)e^{aF(t)}\right)=b_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)e^{aF(t)}\mathrm{d}t+ab_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{3}(t)e^{aF(t)}\mathrm{d}t.

By integrating the equation above, we obtain

u(t)e^{aF(t)}=u(0)e^{aF(0)}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{aF(s)}\bigl(b_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)+ab_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{3}(s)\bigr)\mathrm{d}s.

Noting that
u(0)=\tilde{R}(0)+b_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(0)
and
F(0)=0
gives

\tilde{R}(t)=-b_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)+e^{-aF(t)}\tilde{R}(0)+e^{-aF(t)}b_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(0)+e^{-aF(t)}\int_{0}^{t}e^{aF(s)}\bigl(b_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)+ab_{2}\tilde{\eta}^{3}(s)\bigr)\mathrm{d}s.

(D.102)

Furthermore, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\left(e^{aF(s)}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)\right)=ae^{aF(s)}\tilde{\eta}^{3}(s)+2e^{aF(s)}\tilde{\eta}(s)\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(s),

which implies that

\int_{0}^{t}ae^{aF(s)}\tilde{\eta}^{3}(s)\mathrm{d}s=e^{aF(t)}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)-\tilde{\eta}^{2}(0)-2\int_{0}^{t}e^{aF(s)}\tilde{\eta}(s)\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

(D.103)

Combining (
D.102
) and (
D.103
) gives
the desired result.
∎

Theorem
2
(general version)
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and let
\theta^{p}
be the solution obtained with Algorithm
1
. Assume that

\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\gamma+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}=o_{\gamma}(1).

(D.104)

Consider the schedule

\tilde{\eta}(t)=\frac{\alpha}{t+\tau},\qquad\mbox{with }\alpha:=\frac{3\sqrt{A+90}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}},\quad\tau:=\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits\left(\frac{9(A+90)\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}\gamma,\frac{12\sqrt{2(A+90)}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}}\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),

(D.105)

where
A
is a universal constant s.t.
R(0)+\zeta^{2}/2\leq A
.
Then, by setting
c=3\sqrt{2(A+90)}/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
, we have that, with overwhelming probability,

\mathcal{R}(\theta^{p})=O_{\gamma}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{4}}\gamma+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{4}}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right).

(D.106)

Proof.

Let us introduce the shorthands

\displaystyle\overline{f}(t,\overline{R})

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\overline{R})\overline{R}+\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\overline{R})(\overline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma-2c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t},

\displaystyle\underline{f}(t,\underline{R})

\displaystyle=-2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\tilde{\eta}(t)\mu_{c}(\underline{R})\underline{R}+c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\frac{\nu_{c}(\underline{R})(\underline{R}+\zeta^{2}/2)}{c^{2}}\gamma-2c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}.

Then, consider the auxiliary ODEs

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\overline{R}^{\prime}&=-\overline{a}\tilde{\eta}(t)\overline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\overline{b}_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t-\overline{b}_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{d}t=:\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\overline{a},\overline{b}_{1},\overline{b}_{2}>0,\\
\mathrm{d}\underline{R}^{\prime}&=-\underline{a}\tilde{\eta}(t)\underline{R}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t+\underline{b}_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t-\underline{b}_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{d}t=:\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\underline{a},\underline{b}_{1},\underline{b}_{2}>0,\end{split}

(D.107)

with initial conditions
\overline{R}^{\prime}(0)=\overline{R}(0)=R(0)=\underline{R}(0)=\underline{R}^{\prime}(0)
. By Lemma
D.5
, we have

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)=e^{-\overline{a}F(t)}\left(R(0)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\overline{a}F(s)}\bigl(\overline{b}_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)-2\overline{b}_{2}\tilde{\eta}(s)\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\right),\\
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)=e^{-\underline{a}F(t)}\left(R(0)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\underline{a}F(s)}\bigl(\underline{b}_{1}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)-2\underline{b}_{2}\tilde{\eta}(s)\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(s)\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\right),\end{split}

where

F(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\eta}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

By plugging in the schedule defined in (
D.105
), we obtain

F(t)=\alpha\log\frac{t+\tau}{\tau},\qquad\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{\alpha}{(t+\tau)^{2}}.

Thus, assuming that
\overline{a}\alpha>2
(which will be verified later), we have that

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\left(\frac{\tau}{t+\tau}\right)^{\overline{a}\alpha}\biggl(R(0)&+\overline{b}_{1}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\tau^{\overline{a}\alpha}(\overline{a}\alpha-1)}\left((t+\tau)^{\overline{a}\alpha-1}-\tau^{\overline{a}\alpha-1}\right)\\
&+2\overline{b}_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\tau^{\overline{a}\alpha}(\overline{a}\alpha-2)}\left((t+\tau)^{\overline{a}\alpha-2}-\tau^{\overline{a}\alpha-2}\right)\biggr).\end{split}

This readily gives the upper bound

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)&\leq R(0)+\overline{b}_{1}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(t+\tau)(\overline{a}\alpha-1)}+2\overline{b}_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(t+\tau)^{2}(\overline{a}\alpha-2)}\\
&\leq R(0)+\overline{b}_{1}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\tau(\overline{a}\alpha-1)}+2\overline{b}_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\tau^{2}(\overline{a}\alpha-2)}\end{split}

(D.108)

valid for all
t\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, for
t=1
, we also obtain the upper bound

\begin{split}\overline{R}^{\prime}(1)&\leq\left(\frac{\tau}{1+\tau}\right)^{\overline{a}\alpha}R(0)+\overline{b}_{1}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1+\tau)(\overline{a}\alpha-1)}+2\overline{b}_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(1+\tau)^{2}(\overline{a}\alpha-2)}\\
&\leq\tau^{\overline{a}\alpha}R(0)+\overline{b}_{1}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\overline{a}\alpha-1}+2\overline{b}_{2}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\overline{a}\alpha-2}.\end{split}

(D.109)

Let us now pick

\overline{a}=\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}}{\sqrt{A+90}},\qquad\overline{b}_{1}=\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\frac{c^{2}\gamma}{2},\qquad\overline{b}_{2}=4c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}.

(D.110)

This implies that

\tau=\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits\left(\overline{b}_{1},2\sqrt{\overline{b}_{2}}\right),\qquad\overline{a}\alpha=3,

(D.111)

which combined with (
D.108
) gives

\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq R(0)+\alpha^{2}=R(0)+\frac{9(A+90)}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}.

(D.112)

We now verify that, for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\overline{a}\leq 2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\mu_{c}(\overline{R}(t))=2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{c}{2\sqrt{\overline{R}(t)+\zeta^{2}/2}}\right).

(D.113)

For (
D.113
) to hold, it suffices that

\overline{a}\leq 2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\mathrm{erf}\left(\frac{c}{2\sqrt{A+9(A+90)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}}\right),

(D.114)

due to (
D.112
) and to the fact that
R(0)+\zeta^{2}/2\leq A
. Note that

\mathrm{erf}(z)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{z}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t\geq\frac{2z}{\sqrt{\pi e}}\geq\frac{2z}{9},

where the inequalities holds for all
z\in[0,1/\sqrt{2}]
. Furthermore,
as
c=3\sqrt{2(A+90)}/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}
, one can readily verify that

\frac{c}{2\sqrt{A+9(A+90)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}.

Thus, (
D.114
) is implied by

\overline{a}\leq\frac{2c\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}}{9\sqrt{A+9(A+90)/\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}},

(D.115)

which holds due to the definitions of
c,\overline{a}
and to the fact that
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}\leq 1
.

By Lemma
B.2
, we have that

\frac{2\nu_{c}(R)\left(R+\zeta^{2}/2\right)}{c^{2}}<1,

which implies that

\overline{b}_{1}>\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}c^{2}\frac{\nu_{c}(R)\left(\overline{R}(t)+\zeta^{2}/2\right)}{c^{2}}\gamma,

(D.116)

for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Now, note that
\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)/\mathrm{d}t\leq-2\alpha^{2}/(1+\tau)^{3}<0
for
t\in[0,1]
. Thus,
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
. In fact, if this is not the case, by continuity of
\overline{R}^{\prime}
, there exists an interval
(t^{*},t^{*}+\delta)\subseteq[0,1]
s.t.
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
and
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)<0
for all
t\in(t^{*},t^{*}+\delta)
. However, if
\overline{R}^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
, then the derivative of
\overline{R}^{\prime}
evaluated at
t^{*}
is
>0
, which is a contradiction.

As a result, we have

\overline{f}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime}(t))<\overline{f}^{\prime}(t,\overline{R}^{\prime}(t)),\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in[0,1].

(D.117)

Again, by Lemma
B.2
, we have that

\frac{\mu_{c}(R)}{c}<\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\left(R+\zeta^{2}/2\right)}},\qquad\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)<\frac{2\nu_{c}(R)\left(R+\zeta^{2}/2\right)}{c^{2}}.

Thus, by setting

\begin{split}\underline{a}&=2c\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\zeta^{2}/2}},\qquad\underline{b}_{1}=\frac{c^{2}\gamma\underline{c}_{\nu}(c,\zeta)}{2},\qquad\underline{b}_{2}=c^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}},\end{split}

we have that

\underline{f}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime}(t))>\underline{f}^{\prime}(t,\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)),\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in[0,1].

(D.118)

Note that
\overline{f}(t,R)
and
\underline{f}(t,R)
are continuous in both variables in the intervals
t\in[0,1]
and
R\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, they are also Lipschitz in
R
in these same intervals. Thus, by Theorem 1.3 in
Teschl (2012)
,

\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)<\underline{R}(t),\qquad\overline{R}(t)<\overline{R}^{\prime}(t),\qquad\text{ for all }\,t\in(0,1].

(D.119)

Note that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\tilde{\eta}(t)=\frac{\alpha}{\tau}<\frac{2}{\gamma},

(D.120)

which follows from

\frac{\alpha}{\tau}\leq\frac{3\sqrt{A+90}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{2}}{9\gamma\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}(A+90)}\leq\frac{1}{3\gamma\sqrt{A+90}}\leq\frac{1}{3\gamma\sqrt{90}}.

(D.121)

Thus, noting that

\overline{R}(1)+\frac{c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}=O_{\gamma}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{4}}\gamma+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}^{4}}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right),

the desired result follows from Theorem
1
and Propositions
4.4
and
D.1
.
∎

D.4
Proofs on the minimax rates

In this section, we consider the notation
D=(X,Y)
and
D^{\prime}_{i}=(X^{\prime}_{i},Y^{\prime}_{i})
, with
X,X^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the two neighboring datasets differ in their
i
-th row
(x_{i},y_{i})
and
(x_{i}^{\prime},y_{i}^{\prime})
. Assume that all data satisfies Assumption
1
, i.e.,
(x_{i},y_{i})
are sampled i.i.d. such that
x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
,
y_{i}=x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*}+z_{i}
, and
(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})
is sampled from this same distribution independently. In this section, the asymptotic notation will hide only numerical absolute constants, independent of
\zeta
and
\gamma
.

Denote by
\mathcal{M}
a generic
\rho^{2}/2
-zCDP algorithm.
\mathcal{M}
applied to
D
(or
D^{\prime}
) outputs a random variable in the probability space of the data and the private mechanism.
Let
p_{\mathcal{M}(D)}
(
p_{\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i})}
) be the probability density function of the random variable
\mathcal{M}(D)
(
\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i})
) when taking into account the randomness of
\mathcal{M}
,
X
and
Y
(
X^{\prime}_{i}
and
Y^{\prime}_{i}
).
Define
L_{i}
to be the random variable

\displaystyle L_{i}=\frac{p_{\mathcal{M}(D)}({\theta}^{\prime})}{p_{\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i})}({\theta}^{\prime})},

(D.122)

where
{\theta}^{\prime}
is set to be the random variable
{\theta}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i})
. Then, due to the definition of zCDP (see Definition
3.1
), we have that, for all
D
,
D^{\prime}_{i}
and
\alpha>1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L^{\alpha}_{i}\,|\,D,D^{\prime}_{i}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\rho^{2}}{2}\right).

(D.123)

Thus, taking the expectation with respect to
D
and
D^{\prime}_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L^{\alpha}_{i}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\rho^{2}}{2}\right).

(D.124)

As in
Cai et al. (2021)
, let us define the tracing attack

\displaystyle A_{i}(\theta)=z_{i}x_{i}^{\top}\left(\theta-\theta^{*}\right).

(D.125)

Unless differently stated, in this section, when we use the notation
\mathbb{E}
, we refer to expectations on the randomness of the data
(X,Y)
and of the algorithm
\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma D.6
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and let
\zeta^{2}
be an absolute positive constant.
Then, there exists a prior
\pi
on
\theta^{*}
with support in
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1
and an absolute constant
C>0
such that, for any algorithm
\mathcal{M}
with
\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]<\infty
, either one of the following holds

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]\right]\geq Cd,\qquad\textup{or}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\pi,X,Y,\mathcal{M}}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}(D)-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]>C.

(D.126)

Proof.

Consider a generic algorithm
\mathcal{M}
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[z_{i}x_{i}^{\top}\mathcal{M}(D)\right]=\sum_{j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}(D)_{j}\sum_{i\in[n]}x_{ij}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*})\right],

(D.127)

since the term
\theta^{*}
vanishes in expectation from the definition of
A_{i}
in (
D.125
) and where we replace
z_{i}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*}
and introduce the notation
x_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}
to denote the
j
-th entry of the data vector
x_{i}
.
By Assumption
1
, we have

\displaystyle p(y_{i}|x_{i},\theta^{*})=\mathcal{N}(x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*},\zeta^{2}),\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}p(y_{i}|x_{i},\theta^{*})=\frac{x_{ij}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*})}{\zeta^{2}}p(y_{i}|x_{i},\theta^{*}),

(D.128)

which give

\displaystyle\zeta^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}\log p(y_{i}|x_{i},\theta^{*})

\displaystyle=x_{ij}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*}),

(D.129)

\displaystyle\zeta^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}\log p(Y|X,\theta^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]}x_{ij}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*}),

as
p(Y|X,\theta^{*})=\Pi_{i}p(y_{i}|x_{i},\theta^{*})
. Thus, we have (dropping the dependence of
\mathcal{M}
on
X
and
Y
)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\left[\mathcal{M}_{j}\sum_{i\in[n]}x_{ij}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{j}\zeta^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}\log p(Y|X,\theta^{*})\right]\\
&=\int\mathcal{M}_{j}\zeta^{2}\left(\frac{1}{p(Y|X,\theta^{*})}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}p(Y|X,\theta^{*})\right)p(X)p(Y|X,\theta^{*})p(\mathcal{M}_{j}|X,Y)\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}Y\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{j}\\
&=\int\mathcal{M}_{j}\zeta^{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}p(Y|X,\theta^{*})\right)p(X)p(\mathcal{M}_{j}|X,Y)\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}Y\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{j}\\
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}\int\mathcal{M}_{j}\zeta^{2}p(X)p(Y|X,\theta^{*})p(\mathcal{M}_{j}|X,Y)\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}Y\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}_{j}\\
&=\zeta^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{j}\right],\end{split}

(D.130)

where the fourth line holds since
p(Y|X)
is the only term that depends on
\theta^{*}
, and the derivative is swapped with the integration due to dominated convergence, as in the open ball
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1
we uniformly have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}\log p(Y|X,\theta^{*})\right)^{2}\right]<\infty,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{j}^{2}\right]<\infty.

(D.131)

Here, the first condition follows from Assumption
1
, and the second one by hypothesis. Notice that the previous equation, by Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, implies the condition for completeness in Theorem 2.1 in
Cai et al. (2025)
, and that this condition also implies the function
g_{j}(\theta^{*}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{j}\right]
being differentiable for all
\theta^{*}
in
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1
. Then, plugging (
D.130
) in (
D.127
) and taking the expectation over the prior
\pi
of
\theta^{*}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]\right]=\zeta^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}g_{j}(\theta^{*})\right].

(D.132)

Consider the prior
\pi
on
\theta^{*}
such that all the entries
\theta^{*}_{j}
are independent and distributed according to

\pi_{j}(z)=\frac{15d^{5/2}}{16}\left(\frac{1}{d}-z^{2}\right)^{2},

(D.133)

with support in
z\in(-d^{-1/2},d^{-1/2})
. This prior is such that the support is
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1
, it vanishes at the boundary and

-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\pi_{j}}\left[-z\frac{\pi^{\prime}_{j}(z)}{\pi_{j}(z)}\right]=1,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\pi_{j}}\left[\left(\frac{\pi^{\prime}_{j}(z)}{\pi_{j}(z)}\right)^{2}\right]=10d.

(D.134)

Let us define the one dimensional function

\displaystyle h_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j}):=\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}_{-j}}\left[g_{j}(\theta^{*})\right],

(D.135)

where the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}_{-j}}
takes expectation over all the (independent) entries of
\theta^{*}
, except for the
j
-th. Then, due to (
D.131
), we can apply Stein’s Lemma as stated in Lemma 2.1 in
Cai et al. (2025)
, which yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}g_{j}(\theta^{*})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}_{j}}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}h_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}_{j}}\left[-h_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[-g_{j}(\theta^{*})\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right].

(D.136)

By triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle-g_{j}(\theta^{*})\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\geq-\theta^{*}_{j}\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}-\left|(g_{j}(\theta^{*})-\theta^{*}_{j})\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right|.

(D.137)

Plugging in (
D.136
) and using (
D.134
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}g_{j}(\theta^{*})\right]\geq 1-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\left|(g_{j}(\theta^{*})-\theta^{*}_{j})\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right|\right].

(D.138)

Summing over
j
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{*}_{j}}g_{j}(\theta^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\geq d-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}\left|(g_{j}(\theta^{*})-\theta^{*}_{j})\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right|\right]

(D.139)

\displaystyle\geq d-\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sqrt{\sum_{j\in[d]}\left(g_{j}(\theta^{*})-\theta^{*}_{j}\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j\in[d]}\left(\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq d-\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}\left(g_{j}(\theta^{*})-\theta^{*}_{j}\right)^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}\left(\frac{\pi_{j}^{\prime}(\theta^{*}_{j})}{\pi_{j}(\theta^{*}_{j})}\right)^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle\geq d-\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}(D)\right]-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\sqrt{10d^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq d\left(1-\sqrt{10\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi,X,Y,\mathcal{M}}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}(D)-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\right).

Plugging this inequality in (
D.132
) we get

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]\right]\geq\zeta^{2}d\left(1-\sqrt{10\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi,X,Y,\mathcal{M}}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}(D)-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\right).

(D.140)

Suppose
\mathbb{E}_{\pi,X,Y,\mathcal{M}}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}(D)-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq 1/40
. Then, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]\right]\geq\zeta^{2}d/2
. Thus, the desired result follows by taking
C=\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(\zeta^{2}/2,1/40)
.
∎

Lemma D.7
.

Let Assumption
1
hold,
\zeta^{2}
be an absolute positive constant and
d^{2}/(\rho^{2}n^{2}\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits})=O(1)
. Define
\mathbb{M}
to be the set of all
\rho^{2}/2
-zCDP algorithms such that
\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]<\infty
.
Then, there exists a prior
\pi
on
\theta^{*}
with support in
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1
such that

\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\mathcal{M},X,Y}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]=\Omega\left(\frac{d^{2}}{n^{2}\left(e^{\rho^{2}}-1\right)}\right).

(D.141)

Proof.

Note that, according to (
D.125
) and (
D.122
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i}))\right]=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[(L_{i}-1)A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i}))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[L_{i}A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i}))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right].

(D.142)

The first one holds because
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
and
\left(\mathcal{M}(D_{i}^{\prime})-\theta^{*}\right)
are independent, and the first one is mean-0. The last equality follows from

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L_{i}A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i}))\right]

\displaystyle=\int\frac{p_{\mathcal{M}(D)}(\theta)}{p_{\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i})}(\theta)}A_{i}(\theta)p_{\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i})}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta

(D.143)

\displaystyle=\int A_{i}(\theta)p_{\mathcal{M}(D)}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right].

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[(L_{i}-1)A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i}))\right]

(D.144)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[(L_{i}-1)^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{i}))^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq n\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[(L_{1}-1)^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[A_{1}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{1}))^{2}\right]},

where we used Cauchy-Schwartz in the second line and the fact that the samples are equally distributed in the last line.

Since
\mathbb{E}[L_{1}]=1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(L_{1}-1)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[L_{1}^{2}\right]-1\leq\exp\left(\rho^{2}\right)-1,

(D.145)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[A_{1}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{1}))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[z_{1}^{2}\left(x_{1}^{\top}\left(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{1})-\theta^{*}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\zeta^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{1})-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\zeta^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\mathcal{M}(D)\right)\right],

where the steps in the third line are again motivated by the fact that
z_{1}
,
x_{1}
and
\left(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime}_{1})-\theta^{*}\right)
are independent.

Let us now focus on the LHS of (
D.144
). Suppose

\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\mathcal{M},X,Y}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}(D)-\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\mathcal{M},X,Y}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}}<C_{1}.

(D.146)

Setting
C_{1}
to be the same constant as in Lemma
D.6
, by (
D.126
) we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[A_{i}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]\right]\geq C_{1}d.

(D.147)

This, together with (
D.145
) and (
D.144
), after taking the expectation with respect to
\pi
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\mathcal{M},X,Y}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(\mathcal{M}(D)\right)\right]=\Omega\left(\frac{d^{2}}{n^{2}\left(e^{\rho^{2}}-1\right)}\right),

(D.148)

which yields the desired result.

Suppose instead that

\frac{2\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\mathcal{M},X,Y}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]}{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}}\geq C_{1}.

(D.149)

Then, by hypothesis, we would have

\mathbb{E}_{\pi,\mathcal{M},X,Y}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(D))\right]\geq\frac{\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}C_{1}}{2}=\Omega\left(\frac{d^{2}}{n^{2}\rho^{2}}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{d^{2}}{n^{2}\left(e^{\rho^{2}}-1\right)}\right),

(D.150)

which again yields the desired result.

∎

Lemma D.8
.

Let Assumption
1
hold,
\zeta^{2}
be an absolute positive constant,
d<n=O(d^{2})
,
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits}>0
and
\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}=O(1)
. Define
\mathbb{M}
to be the set of all algorithms such that
\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]<\infty
. Then, we have

\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]=\Omega\left(\frac{d}{n}\right).

(D.151)

Proof.

Let us denote
\pi_{B}
as the Gaussian distribution in
d
dimensions
\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta^{2}/(\lambda n)I)
, with
\lambda=2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}
, truncated at
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}\geq 1
. Then, we have

\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]\geq\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi_{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right].

(D.152)

Let
B
be the closed unit ball, and let
\mathcal{B}
be the ellipsoid
\Sigma^{1/2}B
. Given any algorithm
\mathcal{M}
, define the composition
\mathcal{M}_{B}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\operatorname{Proj}_{\mathcal{B}}\Sigma^{1/2}\circ\mathcal{M}
. This implies that, for all
(X,Y)
,
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,Y)\in B
. Furthermore, for all
(X,Y)
and
\theta^{*}\in B
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,Y)-\theta^{*})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\operatorname{Proj}_{\mathcal{B}}\Sigma^{1/2}\mathcal{M}(X,Y)-\Sigma^{1/2}\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(D.153)

\displaystyle=\left\|\operatorname{Proj}_{\mathcal{B}}\Sigma^{1/2}\mathcal{M}(X,Y)-\operatorname{Proj}_{\mathcal{B}}\Sigma^{1/2}\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\mathcal{M}(X,Y)-\theta^{*}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2},

where the second step holds since
\Sigma^{1/2}\theta^{*}\in\mathcal{B}
, and the last step holds since projections on closed convex sets are non-expansive. Thus, we have
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,Y))\leq\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))
. Then, since the support of
\pi_{B}
is included in
B
, it holds that

\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi_{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]=\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi_{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,Y))\right],

(D.154)

where the equality follows from the fact that the set of algorithms
\{\mathcal{M}_{B}\,|\,\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}\}
is a subset of
\mathbb{M}
.

Let us denote with
\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta^{2}/(\lambda n)I)
the non-truncated Gaussian distribution and with
A
the event that
\theta^{*}\sim\pi
is such that
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1
. Then, dropping the notation
\mathbb{M}
and the dependence of
\mathcal{M}
from
(X,Y)
, one has

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi_{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(A)}\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\mathbf{1}_{A}\right]

(D.155)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(A)}\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})(1-\mathbf{1}_{A^{c}})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(A)}\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\right]-\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(A)}\sup_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\mathbf{1}_{A^{c}}\right].

Note that

\sup_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\mathbf{1}_{A^{c}}\right]\leq\sup_{\mathcal{M}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})^{2}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(A^{c})}\leq 2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(A^{c})},

(D.156)

where the first step follows from Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and the second step holds since
\left\|\mathcal{M}_{B}\right\|_{2}\leq 1
. Furthermore, since
\lambda>2
and
n>d
, due to concentration of the norm of Gaussian vectors we have that
\mathbb{P}(A)\geq 1-2e^{-c_{1}d}
for some absolute constant
c_{1}
. Then, we also have

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M})\right]

(D.157)

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta^{2}}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left((X^{\top}X/n+\lambda I)^{-1}\Sigma\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Sigma^{-1/2}(X^{\top}X/n+\lambda I)\Sigma^{-1/2}\right]\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta^{2}}{n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\left(I+\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2\lambda n}\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits\left(\Sigma\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta^{2}d}{4\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}n},

where the second step follows from the closed form of the Bayes estimator under Gaussian prior (see, e.g., Eq. (63) in
Mourtada (2022)
), the third step follows from Jensen inequality since the mapping
A\mapsto\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(A^{-1})
is convex if
A
is positive definite (see Eq. (1.33) in
Bhatia (2007)
), and the last two steps follow from the fact that
\lambda
has been set to
2\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}
and
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits(\Sigma)=d
.

Then, putting (
D.155
), (
D.156
) and (
D.157
) together, we get

\inf_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{*}\sim\pi_{B}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}_{B})\right]\geq\frac{\zeta^{2}d}{4\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}n}-4\lambda_{\mathop{\rm max}\nolimits}e^{-c_{1}d/2}.

(D.158)

Since
n=O(d^{2})
, merging with (
D.152
), (
D.154
) yields the desired result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Note that, if
\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
diverges, then
\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]
diverges as well.
Thus, let us focus on the class of
\rho^{2}/2
-zCDP algorithms such that
\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{M}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]<\infty
. By Lemmas
D.7
and
D.8
, and using the same argument as in (
D.152
), we have

\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]=\Omega\left(\frac{d}{n}+\frac{d^{2}}{(e^{\rho^{2}}-1)n^{2}}\right).

(D.159)

Notice that, if
\rho>1
,

\frac{d^{2}}{(e^{\rho^{2}}-1)n^{2}}=O\left(\frac{d}{n}\right),\qquad\frac{d^{2}}{\rho^{2}n^{2}}=O\left(\frac{d}{n}\right),

(D.160)

i.e., the first terms in the RHS of (
D.162
) and (
D.159
) dominate. On the other hand, if
\rho<1
, we have

\frac{d^{2}}{(e^{\rho^{2}}-1)n^{2}}=\Theta\left(\frac{d^{2}}{\rho^{2}n^{2}}\right).

(D.161)

Thus, for any value of
\rho>0
, we have that

\inf_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{M}}\sup_{\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}<1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{M}(X,Y))\right]=\Omega\left(\frac{d}{n}+\frac{d^{2}}{n^{2}\rho^{2}}\right),

(D.162)

which gives the desired result when taking sequentially the limits
d/n\to\gamma
and
\gamma\to 0
. ∎

Appendix E
Proofs for Section
6

In this section, we always assume Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
to hold. We consider the limit
\gamma\to 0
, and use the notation
f(\gamma)\asymp g(\gamma)
to indicate two quantities such that
f(\gamma)=\Theta_{\gamma}(g(\gamma))
, where the notation
\Theta_{\gamma}
does not track constants depending on
\phi
,
\psi
,
\zeta
,
\left\|\theta^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}
, and
\alpha
. Similarly, we will consider the notations
\gtrsim
and
\lesssim
for
\Omega_{\gamma}(\cdot)
and
O_{\gamma}(\cdot)
respectively.
Furthermore, unless differently specified, we will always consider schedules such that
\tilde{\eta}(t)\leq 2/\gamma
uniformly in
t
, so that
\bar{\eta}(t)=\tilde{\eta}(t)
.

We have

\Gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\eta}(s)\mu_{c}(R(s))\mathrm{d}s,\qquad F(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\eta}(s)\mathrm{d}s,

(E.1)

where we defined the shorthand
F(t)
. Then, (
6.2
) reads

R(t)=\mathcal{F}(\Gamma(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)\nu_{c}(R(s))(R(s)+\zeta^{2}/2)\gamma\mathcal{K}(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s))+2c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s))\right)\mathrm{d}s.

(E.2)

Within this section, for convenience, we also introduce the shorthand
v=c\tilde{\eta}(0)
.

Proposition E.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}(x)
,
\mathcal{K}(x)
,
\mathcal{J}(x)
be defined according to (
6.3
). Consider the shorthands

\kappa_{1}=2-\phi-\psi,\qquad\kappa_{2}=3-\phi,\qquad\kappa_{3}=2-\phi,

(E.3)

so that
\kappa_{1}>1
,
\kappa_{2}>2
, and
\kappa_{3}>1
due to Assumption
3
.
Then, we have that
\mathcal{F}(x)\asymp x^{-\kappa_{1}}
,
\mathcal{K}(x)\asymp x^{-\kappa_{2}}
, and
\mathcal{J}(x)\asymp x^{-\kappa_{3}}
uniformly for
x\geq 1
, and
\mathcal{F}(x)\asymp\mathcal{K}(x)\asymp\mathcal{J}(x)\asymp 1
uniformly for
x<1
.

Proof.

Due to Assumption
3
, we have that, for
d\to\infty
,

\mathcal{F}(x)\to\int p(\lambda)\lambda^{-\psi+1}e^{-2\lambda x}\mathrm{d}\lambda,\qquad\mathcal{K}(x)\to\int p(\lambda)\lambda^{2}e^{-2\lambda x}\mathrm{d}\lambda,\qquad\mathcal{J}(x)\to\int p(\lambda)\lambda e^{-2\lambda x}\mathrm{d}\lambda.

(E.4)

Then, for the second part of the statement, notice that
\mathcal{F}(x)
,
\mathcal{K}(x)
and
\mathcal{J}(x)
are decreasing in
x
, and a direct computation for
x\in\{0,1\}
yields the desired result. For
x\geq 1
, the desired result is given by the same approach used in Lemma D.5 in
Collins-Woodfin et al. (2024b)
.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

Assume
c\lesssim 1
and
\tilde{\eta}(t)\leq 2/\gamma
uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
. Then, we have

\displaystyle R(t)

\displaystyle\asymp\mathcal{F}(\Gamma(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s))+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s))\right)\mathrm{d}s

(E.5)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(cF(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(cF(t)).

Furthermore, if
R(t)
is uniformly upper bounded by a constant not dependent on
\gamma
, we have

R(t)\asymp\mathcal{F}(cF(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s.

(E.6)

Proof.

By Lemma
B.2
, we have that
\mu_{c}(R)\lesssim c
. Then, multiplying both sides by
\tilde{\eta}(s)
and integrating from
s
to
t
, we get

\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s)\lesssim c\left(F(t)-F(s)\right),

(E.7)

and
\Gamma(t)\lesssim cF(t)
when setting
s=0
. Then, since
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{J}
are all uniformly
\asymp
to decreasing functions, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\Gamma(t))

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(cF(t))

(E.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s)\right)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{K}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\left(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s)\right)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{J}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right).

Furthermore, again due to Lemma
B.2
, since
c\lesssim 1
, we have
c^{2}\asymp\nu_{c}(R(s))(R(s)+\zeta^{2}/2)
. Thus, since all terms on the RHS of (
E.2
) are positive, and dropping numerical constants, we get the first part of the thesis.

For the second part of the thesis, notice that Lemma
B.2
gives

\frac{c}{\sqrt{\zeta^{2}+2R}}\lesssim\mu_{c}(R)\lesssim c,

(E.9)

which implies that, if
\sup_{t}R(t)\lesssim 1
, the relations
\lesssim
in (
E.7
) can be replaced by
\asymp
. Thus, the thesis follows from Proposition
E.1
, since we have
\mathcal{F}(x)\asymp\mathcal{F}(ax)
if
a\asymp 1
, with the same relation holding for
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{J}
.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

Assume
c\lesssim 1
and
\tilde{\eta}(t)\leq 2/\gamma
uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
. Then, if

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(cF(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s\lesssim 1,

(E.10)

uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
, we have that

\displaystyle R(t)\asymp\mathcal{F}(cF(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s.

(E.11)

Proof.

Define the following system of ODEs

\mathrm{d}D_{i}^{\prime}(t)=-\lambda_{i}\tilde{\eta}(t)cD^{\prime}_{i}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{i}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)c^{2}\gamma\mathrm{d}t+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(E.12)

with
D^{\prime}_{i}(0)=D_{i}(0)=d\left(\omega_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*}\right)^{2}/2
, and
R^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}D^{\prime}_{i}(t)
. Then, following the same steps as in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) we have that
R^{\prime}(t)
is equal to the LHS in (
E.10
).

By hypothesis, there exists a large enough constant
C_{1}
such that
R^{\prime}(t)\leq C_{1}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Then, consider the new system of ODEs

\mathrm{d}\underline{D}_{i}^{\prime}(t)=-\lambda_{i}\tilde{\eta}(t)\sqrt{2C_{1}+\zeta^{2}}\frac{c}{\sqrt{2\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)+\zeta^{2}}}\underline{D}^{\prime}_{i}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\lambda_{i}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(t)c^{2}\gamma\mathrm{d}t+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(t)\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

(E.13)

with
\underline{D}^{\prime}_{i}(0)=D^{\prime}_{i}(0)
, and
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\underline{D}^{\prime}_{i}(t)
. Then, by ODE comparison, we have
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\leq R^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in[0,1]
. This follows from the same argument as in Theorem 1.3 in
Teschl (2012)
, since
R^{\prime}(t)
(
\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)
) is non-decreasing in
D_{i}^{\prime}(t)
(
\underline{D}_{i}^{\prime}(t)
) for all
i
.

Due to Lemma
B.2
, since
c\lesssim 1
, we have that there exists a sufficiently large constant
C_{2}
such that
\mu_{c}(R)C_{2}\geq c/\sqrt{2R+\zeta^{2}}
. Then, by ODE comparison, integrating as in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), and denoting
C_{3}=C_{2}\sqrt{2C_{1}+\zeta^{2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}(C_{3}\Gamma(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}\left(C_{3}\Gamma(t)-C_{3}\Gamma(s)\right)+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}\left(C_{3}\Gamma(t)-C_{3}\Gamma(s)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s

(E.14)

\displaystyle\asymp R(t),

where the last step holds since
c^{2}\asymp\nu_{c}(R(s))(R(s)+\zeta^{2}/2)
(due to Lemma
B.2
since
c\lesssim 1
), and since
\mathcal{F}(x)\asymp\mathcal{F}(ax)
if
a\asymp 1
(with the same relation holding for
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{J}
) due to Proposition
E.1
.

Thus, we have

R(t)\gtrsim R^{\prime}(t)\geq\underline{R}^{\prime}(t)\gtrsim R(t),

(E.15)

where the first inequality is a consequence of Lemma
E.2
, which directly yields
R(t)\asymp R^{\prime}(t)
, proving the thesis.
∎

Lemma E.4
.

Let
\tilde{\eta}(t)=\tilde{\eta}(0)(1-t)^{\alpha}
, with
\alpha\in\{0,1/2\}
or
\alpha\geq 1
. Assume
c\lesssim 1
,
\tilde{\eta}(0)\leq 2/\gamma
,
v=\tilde{\eta}(0)c=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
.
Then, we have that

R(1)+\frac{2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\gtrsim v^{-\kappa_{1}}+\gamma v^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}g(v),

(E.16)

where we introduced

g(v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle v^{\frac{2}{\alpha+1}}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\phi<\frac{2}{\alpha+1},\\
\displaystyle v^{\phi}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\phi>\frac{2}{\alpha+1},\\
\displaystyle v^{\phi}\ln v&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\phi=\frac{2}{\alpha+1}.\end{array}\right.

(E.17)

Furthermore, the relation
\gtrsim
in (
E.16
) becomes
\asymp
in the following cases:
(i)

\alpha=0
, or
(ii)

\alpha=1/2
and
\gamma^{2}v^{4/3}/\rho^{2}\lesssim 1
, or
(iii)

\alpha\geq 1
and
\gamma^{2}v/\rho^{2}\lesssim 1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
E.2
, we have that

R(t)\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(cF(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)c^{2}\gamma\mathcal{K}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}\left(cF(t)-cF(s)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s,

(E.18)

where
\gtrsim
can be replaced by
\asymp
if the RHS is shown to be
\lesssim 1
for all
t\in[0,1]
, due to Lemma
E.3
.

Let us focus on the first term of the RHS of (
E.18
). Due to (
5.1
), (
E.1
) gives

cF(t)=\frac{v}{(\alpha+1)}(1-(1-t)^{\alpha+1}).

(E.19)

Then, due to Proposition
E.1
, we have

\mathcal{F}(cF(1))\asymp v^{-\kappa_{1}},

(E.20)

with
\mathcal{F}(cF(t))\lesssim 1
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Let us now focus on the the second term of the RHS of (
E.18
), i.e.,

N_{2}(t):=\gamma c^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)\mathcal{K}(cF(t)-cF(s))\mathrm{d}s.

(E.21)

Consider the change of variable
u=cF(t)-cF(s)
. This yields

N_{2}(t)=\gamma c\int_{0}^{cF(t)}\tilde{\eta}(s(u))\mathcal{K}(u)\mathrm{d}u.

(E.22)

Furthermore, (
E.19
) yields

u=\frac{v}{\alpha+1}\left((1-s)^{\alpha+1}-(1-t)^{\alpha+1}\right),\qquad s=1-\left(\frac{u(\alpha+1)}{v}+(1-t)^{\alpha+1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}.

(E.23)

Then, plugging the last relation in (
5.1
) we get

c\tilde{\eta}(s(u))=v\left(\frac{u(\alpha+1)}{v}+(1-t)^{\alpha+1}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}.

(E.24)

Plugging this in (
E.22
) we obtain

N_{2}(t)=\gamma v^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\int_{0}^{cF(t)}\left(u(\alpha+1)+v(1-t)^{\alpha+1}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}\mathcal{K}(u)\mathrm{d}u.

(E.25)

Using Proposition
E.1
, and the fact that
cF(1)\asymp v=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
, we have

\displaystyle N_{2}(1)

\displaystyle\asymp\gamma v^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}u^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}\mathcal{K}(u)\mathrm{d}u+\int_{1}^{cF(1)}u^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}\mathcal{K}(u)\mathrm{d}u\right)

(E.26)

\displaystyle\asymp\gamma v^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}u^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}\mathrm{d}u+\int_{1}^{cF(1)}u^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}u^{-\kappa_{2}}\mathrm{d}u\right)

\displaystyle\asymp\gamma v^{\frac{1}{\alpha+1}},

where the last line holds since
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}-\kappa_{2}<-1
for any
\alpha\geq 0
, since
\kappa_{2}>2
. Notice that, with a similar computation, we also have

N_{2}(t)\lesssim\gamma v\lesssim 1,

(E.27)

uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
, since
c\lesssim 1
and
\tilde{\eta}(0)\leq 2/\gamma
by hypothesis.

Let us now focus on the third term in the RHS of (
E.18
) for
\alpha>0
:

N_{3}(t):=\gamma^{2}c^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\mathcal{J}(cF(t)-cF(s))\mathrm{d}s,

(E.28)

where we have

\rho^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)=2\alpha\tilde{\eta}(0)^{2}(1-s)^{2\alpha-1}.

(E.29)

The change of variable
u=cF(t)-cF(s)
yields

N_{3}(t)=\gamma^{2}c\int_{0}^{cF(t)}\frac{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s(u))}{\tilde{\eta}(s(u))}\mathcal{J}(u)\mathrm{d}u\asymp\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v\int_{0}^{cF(t)}(1-s(u))^{\alpha-1}\mathcal{J}(u)\mathrm{d}u,

(E.30)

which gives, plugging in (
E.23
),

N_{3}(t)\asymp\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v\int_{0}^{cF(t)}\left(\frac{u}{v}+(1-t)^{\alpha+1}\right)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}\mathcal{J}(u)\mathrm{d}u.

(E.31)

Using Proposition
E.1
, we have

\displaystyle N_{3}(1)

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v^{\frac{2}{\alpha+1}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}u^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}\mathcal{J}(u)\mathrm{d}u+\int_{1}^{cF(1)}u^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}\mathcal{J}(u)\mathrm{d}u\right)

(E.32)

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v^{\frac{2}{\alpha+1}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}u^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}\mathrm{d}u+\int_{1}^{cF(1)}u^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}u^{-\kappa_{3}}\mathrm{d}u\right).

Notice that, since
cF(1)\asymp v=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
and
\kappa_{3}=2-\phi
, we have

\int_{1}^{cF(1)}u^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}u^{-\kappa_{3}}\mathrm{d}u\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle 1&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi<1,\\
\displaystyle v^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi-1}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi>1,\\
\displaystyle\ln v&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi=1.\end{array}\right.

(E.33)

This yields, since
\int_{0}^{1}u^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}}\mathrm{d}u\asymp 1
for
\alpha>0
,

N_{3}(1)\asymp\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v^{\frac{2}{\alpha+1}}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi<1,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v^{\phi}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi>1,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}v^{\phi}\ln v&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}+\phi=1.\end{array}\right.

(E.34)

Thus, if
\alpha\geq 1
, it is sufficient to have
\gamma^{2}v/\rho^{2}\lesssim 1
to guarantee that
N_{3}(1)\lesssim 1
(recall that
\phi<1)
. Notice that, a direct computation from (
E.31
) shows that this condition is sufficient to guarantee
N_{3}(t)\lesssim 1
uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
.

In the case
\alpha=1/2
, only the first case in (
E.34
) is possible, and this gives the condition
\gamma^{2}v^{4/3}/\rho^{2}\lesssim 1
to guarantee that
N_{3}(1)\lesssim 1
(and this is also sufficient to guarantee
N_{3}(t)\lesssim 1
uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
).

Thus, the first part of the thesis follows from plugging (
E.20
), (
E.26
) and (
E.34
) in (
E.18
), since for
\alpha=0
we have
v=c\tilde{\eta}(1)
, and
\tilde{\eta}(1)=0
for
\alpha>0
.

The second part of the thesis follows from the fact that the RHS of (
E.18
) is uniformly
\lesssim 1
due to (
E.20
) and (
E.27
) for
(i)

\alpha=0
, or
(ii)

\alpha=1/2
and
\gamma^{2}v^{4/3}/\rho^{2}\lesssim 1
, or
(iii)

\alpha\geq 1
and
\gamma^{2}v/\rho^{2}\lesssim 1
, due to the discussion following (
E.34
).
∎

Lemma E.5
.

Let
\tilde{\eta}(t)=\tilde{\eta}(0)(1-t)^{\alpha}
, with
\alpha=\{0,1/2\}
and
\alpha\geq 1
, such that
\tilde{\eta}(0)\leq 2/\gamma
, and denote
v=\tilde{\eta}(0)c
.
Assume
\rho\asymp\gamma^{b}
with
b<1
independent of
\gamma
.

Suppose
\phi(\alpha+1)<2
. Set
c\lesssim 1
and
c\tilde{\eta}(0)=\gamma^{a}
, with

a=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha+1}{K+1}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle b\leq\frac{K}{2(K+1)},\\
\displaystyle-\frac{2(1-b)(\alpha+1)}{K+2}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle b>\frac{K}{2(K+1)},\end{array}\right.

(E.35)

where
K=(2-\phi-\psi)(\alpha+1)
. Then, we have that
R(1)+2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}\asymp\gamma^{h}
, with

h=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle\frac{K}{K+1}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle b\leq\frac{K}{2(K+1)},\\
\displaystyle\frac{2K(1-b)}{K+2}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle b>\frac{K}{2(K+1)}.\end{array}\right.

(E.36)

Suppose
\phi(\alpha+1)\geq 2
. Set
c\lesssim 1
, and
c\tilde{\eta}(0)=\gamma^{a}
. If

b\leq 1-\frac{(\kappa_{1}+\phi)(\alpha+1)}{2(K+1)},

(E.37)

then set
a
as in the first case of (
E.35
) to achieve the same
h
as in the first case of (
E.36
). If (
E.37
) does not hold, then set

a=-\frac{2(1-b)}{\kappa_{1}+\phi},

(E.38)

which gives
R(1)+2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}\asymp\gamma^{h}
, with

h=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle\frac{2\kappa_{1}(1-b)}{\kappa_{1}+\phi}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\phi(\alpha+1)>2,\\
\displaystyle\frac{2\kappa_{1}(1-b)}{\kappa_{1}+\phi}+\frac{\ln(a\ln\gamma)}{\ln\gamma}&\mbox{if }\;\displaystyle\phi(\alpha+1)=2.\end{array}\right.

(E.39)

Furthermore, for each value of
\alpha
, we have
R(1)+2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}\gtrsim\gamma^{h}
for all choices of
c
and all choices of
a
independent of
\gamma
.

Proof.

Consider the case
c=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
. Due to Lemma
B.2
and the same argument used in (
D.44
), we have that

\displaystyle R(t)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(\Gamma(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)\gamma\mathcal{K}(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s))+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}(\Gamma(t)-\Gamma(s))\right)\mathrm{d}s

(E.40)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(F(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)\gamma\mathcal{K}(F(t)-F(s))+c^{2}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}(F(t)-F(s))\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{F}(F(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\eta}^{2}(s)\gamma\mathcal{K}(F(t)-F(s))+\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(s)\gamma^{2}\mathcal{J}(F(t)-F(s))\right)\mathrm{d}s,

where the the second step holds for an argument similar to the one in the proof of Lemma
E.2
, as
\mu_{c}(R)<1
. Then, the risk at all times is lower bounded by an expression which is identical in form to the setting
c\asymp 1
, which implies that the smallest rates for
R(1)+2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}/\rho^{2}
can be achieved for
c\lesssim 1
. Furthermore, if
c\lesssim 1
and
v\lesssim 1
, (
E.20
) gives
R(1)\gtrsim 1
, which is a vacuous rate.

Then, let us focus on
c\lesssim 1
and
v=\omega_{\gamma}(1)
. Then, Lemma
E.4
gives the lower bound in (
E.16
). During the proof, we will suppose this bound to be tight, and we will find the value of
a
(with
v=\gamma^{a}
) that minimizes this lower bound. Later, we will verify that
a\in[-1,0)
(such that it is possible to consider
c\lesssim 1
and
\tilde{\eta}(0)<2/\gamma
),
2-2b+4a/3\geq 0
for
\alpha=1/2
, and
2-2b+a\geq 0
for
\alpha\geq 1
, guaranteeing that indeed the lower bound in (
E.16
) is tight and that the choice of
a
is optimal.

•

Suppose
\phi(\alpha+1)<2
. Then, Lemma
E.4
gives

R(1)+\frac{2c^{2}\tilde{\eta}^{2}(1)\gamma^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\gtrsim\gamma^{e_{1}}+\gamma^{e_{2}}+\gamma^{e_{3}},

(E.41)

with

e_{1}=-a\kappa_{1},\qquad e_{2}=\frac{a}{\alpha+1}+1,\qquad e_{3}=2-2b+\frac{2a}{\alpha+1}.

(E.42)

Then, the problem of hyper-parameter optimization reduces to finding
a
such that
\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
is maximized, giving the minimum lower bound on the risk.
Consider the value of
a
for which
e_{1}=e_{2}
. This gives

a=-\frac{\alpha+1}{K+1}\in[-1,0],

(E.43)

where the inclusion follows from
\alpha\geq 0
and
K>\alpha
since
\kappa_{1}>1
. Note that, for this value of
a
, if
b\leq\frac{K}{2(K+1)}
, then we have

e_{1}=e_{2}=\frac{K}{K+1}\leq e_{3}.

(E.44)

Then, since
e_{1}
is monotonically decreasing in
a
and
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
are increasing in
a
(see Figures
11
,
13
and
13
for a graphical presentation of this case), (
E.43
) gives the optimal choice of
a
and (
E.44
) the corresponding lower bound on the risk. Furthermore, note that, in this case, we have

2-2b\geq\frac{K+2}{K+1}=1+\frac{1}{K+1}=1-\frac{a}{\alpha+1}.

(E.45)

Then, for
\alpha\geq 1
this gives
2-2b+a\geq 0
, and for
\alpha=1/2
this gives
2-2b+4a/3\geq 0
, which implies that (
E.41
) is tight, due to the second part of the thesis of Lemma
E.4
.

If
b>\frac{K}{2(K+1)}
, the value of
a
in (
E.43
) is such that
e_{1}=e_{2}\geq e_{3}
. Then, set
e_{1}=e_{3}
, which yields

a=-\frac{2(1-b)(\alpha+1)}{K+2}\in[-1,0],

(E.46)

where the inclusion follows from
b\leq 1
and
\kappa_{1}>1
. Note that, for this value of
a
, since
b>\frac{K}{2(K+1)}
we have

e_{1}=e_{3}=\frac{2K(1-b)}{K+2}\leq e_{2}.

(E.47)

Then, since
e_{1}
is monotonically decreasing in
a
and
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
are increasing in
a
(see Figure
11
for a graphical presentation of this case), (
E.46
) gives the optimal choice of
a
and (
E.47
) the corresponding lower bound on the risk. Furthermore, in this case, we have that

2-2b+a=\frac{2(1-b)(K+1-\alpha)}{K+2}>0,

(E.48)

where the last step holds since
K>\alpha
. If
\alpha=1/2
, we have that
2-2b+4a/3>0
since
3\kappa_{1}/2+2>3/2
, which implies that (
E.41
) is tight due to the second part of the thesis of Lemma
E.4
. This concludes the argument for the case
\phi(\alpha+1)<2
.

Figure 10:
Plots of
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
as in (
E.42
) as a function of
a
for
\kappa_{1}=1.5
,
\alpha=0
,
b=0.25
. Privacy comes for free, since
\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}))=\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2}))
.

Figure 11:
Plots of
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
as in (
E.42
) as a function of
a
for
\kappa_{1}=1.5
,
\alpha=0
,
b=0.4
. Privacy comes with a performance cost, since
\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}))=\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{3}))
.

•

Suppose
\phi(\alpha+1)>2
. Then, Lemma
E.4
gives that (
E.41
) holds with

e_{1}=-a\kappa_{1},\qquad e_{2}=\frac{a}{\alpha+1}+1,\qquad e_{3}=2-2b+a\phi.

(E.49)

As before, set
a
for which
e_{1}=e_{2}
. This gives the result in (
E.43
), and if

b\leq 1-\frac{(\kappa_{1}+\phi)(\alpha+1)}{2(K+1)},

(E.50)

we have that (
E.44
) holds. Notice that the case
\phi(\alpha+1)>2
cannot happen for either
\phi\leq 0
or
\alpha\leq 1
, and for
\alpha>1
, we have

2-2b+a\geq\frac{(\kappa_{1}+\phi)(\alpha+1)}{K+1}+a=\frac{(\kappa_{1}+\phi-1)(\alpha+1)}{K+1}=\frac{(1-\psi)(\alpha+1)}{K+1}>0,

(E.51)

where the last step holds since
1-\psi>\phi
by Assumption
3
. Then, this implies that (
E.41
) is tight due to the second part of the thesis of Lemma
E.4
.

If (
E.50
) does not hold, the value of
a
in (
E.43
) is such that
e_{1}=e_{2}\geq e_{3}
. Then, set
e_{1}=e_{3}
, which yields

a=-\frac{2(1-b)}{\kappa_{1}+\phi}\in[-1,0],

(E.52)

where the inclusion follows from
b\leq 1
and the lower bound on
b
. Notice that, for this value of
a
and interval of
b
we have

e_{1}=e_{3}=\frac{2\kappa_{1}(1-b)}{\kappa_{1}+\phi}\leq e_{2}.

(E.53)

Then, since
e_{1}
is monotonically decreasing in
a
and
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
are increasing in
a
,
(
E.52
) gives the optimal choice of
a
and (
E.53
) the corresponding lower bound on the risk. Furthermore, in this case, we have that

2-2b+a=\frac{2(1-b)(\kappa_{1}+\phi-1)}{\kappa_{1}+\phi}>0,

(E.54)

where the last step holds since
\phi>0
, which implies that (
E.41
) is tight due to the second part of the thesis of Lemma
E.4
. This proves the thesis for the case
\phi(\alpha+1)>2
.

•

Suppose
\phi(\alpha+1)=2
. Then, Lemma
E.4
gives that (
E.41
) holds with

e_{1}=-a\kappa_{1},\qquad e_{2}=\frac{a}{\alpha+1}+1,\qquad e_{3}=2-2b+a\phi-\frac{\ln(|a|\ln(1/\gamma))}{\ln(1/\gamma)}.

(E.55)

Since the last term on the RHS of the last expression is
o_{\gamma}(1)
, the case given by (
E.50
) is identical to the previous one.

When instead (
E.50
) does not hold, setting
a
as in (
E.52
)
is also optimal as
\gamma\to 0
, since
\ln(1/\gamma)=\omega_{\gamma}\left(\ln(|a|\ln(1/\gamma)\right)
. Thus, the desired result follows.

∎

) as a function of

for

,

,

. Privacy comes for free, since
\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}))=\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2}))
.

,

and

as in (

) as a function of

for
\kappa_{1}=1.5
,
\alpha=10
,
b=0.4
. Privacy comes for free, since
\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}))=\mathop{\rm arg\,max}\limits(\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(e_{1},e_{2}))
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

The proof is a direct consequence of Lemma
E.5
together with Theorem
1
and Proposition
4.4
, after replacing
\kappa_{1}=2-\phi-\psi
. ∎

Proof of Corollary
6.1
.

If
\phi\leq 0
, for any
\alpha\geq 0
we have that
\phi(\alpha+1)\leq 0<2
. Then, taking
\alpha\to\infty
(and consequently
K\to\infty
as defined in Lemma
E.5
),
h
as defined in (
E.36
) converges (from below) to
\mathop{\rm min}\nolimits(1,2(1-b))
, which gives the first part of the thesis.

If
\phi>0
, it is still desirable to consider the limit
\alpha\to\infty
, as
h
is non-decreasing in
\alpha
(this can be seen from Lemma
E.4
). Then, this setting reduces to the case
\phi(\alpha+1)>2
. As
\alpha\to\infty
, we have that the threshold in (
E.50
) approaches

b^{*}=1-\frac{\kappa_{1}+\phi}{2\kappa_{1}}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\phi}{2(2-\phi-\psi)}

(E.56)

from above. Furthermore, the exponent
h
approaches 1 from below if
b\leq b^{*}
, and it is equal to
2(1-b)\frac{2-\phi-\psi}{2-\psi}
otherwise, which concludes the proof. ∎
